-\section{Causal-Effect Shifts under Joint Interventions}
-\label{app:ce-shift-rainclouds}
+This appendix provides distributional and observation-level views that complement the selected figures in Chapter~\ref{sec:results}. The sections follow the order of the results chapter, and each figure shows one dataset--model combination.
 
-Figure~\ref{fig:appendix-ce-shift-rainclouds} complements the faithfulness distributions in Figure~\ref{fig:question-level-faithfulness-rainclouds}. For each question, the plotted value is the arithmetic mean of the concept-level CE values. The atomic and interaction-aware values originate from the same joint FELICS run as described in Section \ref{sec:controlled_atomic_baseline}. Thus, extracted concepts and responses are fixed within each comparison.
+\section{Distributions of Question-Level Faithfulness and CE}
+\label{app:distributions}
+
+Figures~\ref{fig:app-dist-f-bbq-gptoss} to~\ref{fig:app-dist-ce-credit-mistral} show the distributions behind the dataset-level values in Table~\ref{tab:results-dataset-overview}. Each panel combines a density estimate (cloud), one point per evaluated question (rain), and a horizontal boxplot (umbrella) whose center line marks the median. The faithfulness panels additionally report the dataset-level estimate $\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{X})$. For CE, the plotted value of each question is the arithmetic mean of its concept-level CE values. The atomic and interaction-aware values originate from the same joint FELICS run as described in Section~\ref{sec:controlled_atomic_baseline}. Thus, extracted concepts and responses are fixed within each comparison. The CE axes are shared between the two models of a dataset but differ across datasets because their CE magnitudes differ substantially.

+\section{Oracle Co-Grouping Rates for BBQ}
+\label{app:oracle-heatmap-bbq}
 
-Figures~\ref{fig:appendix-ce-shift-identity-natural-language} and~\ref{fig:appendix-ce-shift-identity-credit} provide a paired robustness view of the CE distributions in Figure~\ref{fig:appendix-ce-shift-rainclouds}. Each point represents one question from the same joint FELICS run. Its coordinates are the atomic and joint question-level means of causal effects assigned to concepts as in Section \ref{sec:controlled_atomic_baseline}, marker area increases with the number of concepts contributing to both means. $k$ in the caption denotes the range of the number of concepts per question within the dataset and model. For New German Credit all questions contains the same 12 concepts, so marker size is constant. The identity line shows whether questions with high atomic CE also retain high interaction-aware CE.
+Figure~\ref{fig:oracle-heatmap-bbq} complements the MedQA heat map in Section~\ref{sec:results-oracle-behavior} and uses the same construction. Most BBQ category combinations rest on fewer than ten eligible pairs, so their rates are only indicative.
 
-The upper-tail questions generally remain comparatively high under both attribution methods. The marker sizes do not reveal a consistent tendency for questions with more concepts to have larger mean CE; for New German Credit, the concept count is fixed and cannot account for the observed variation.
+\begin{figure}[H]
+  \centering
+  \begin{adjustbox}{max width=\textwidth}
+    \OracleHeatmapBBQ
+  \end{adjustbox}
+  \caption[Oracle co-grouping rates for BBQ]{Co-grouping rates of the BBQ oracle on LLM-normalized categories, pooled across both evaluated models. Gray cells mark category combinations that never occurred together in an evaluated question.}
+  \label{fig:oracle-heatmap-bbq}
+\end{figure}

+\section{Agreement between Implementations}
+\label{app:rq2-identity}
+
+Figures~\ref{fig:app-rq2-f-bbq-gptoss} to~\ref{fig:app-rq2-ce-credit-gptoss} complement Section~\ref{sec:results-original-validation} with the identity plots of FELICS at $n_{\max}=1$ and the original implementation. Each point corresponds to a question available in both independently sampled runs, the vertical axis represents FELICS, and the dashed line denotes exact agreement. The CE coordinates are the question-level means over the concepts retained in the respective run, as used by the equivalence and paired-difference tests. Faithfulness estimates outside the plotted range are drawn as triangles on the border.
+

 \section{Concept-Level Effects of Shapley Normalization}
 \label{app:rq3-normalization-identity}
 
-Figures~\ref{fig:appendix-rq3-normalization-natural-language} and~\ref{fig:appendix-rq3-normalization-credit} complement the rank-correlation analysis for Research Question~\ref{rq:ranking} from Section \ref{sec:results-ranking-preservation}. Each point is one concept in one question, with the raw Shapley CE on the horizontal axis and the adjusted Shapley CE after normalization on the vertical axis. The two variables are defined in Section~\ref{sec:methodology-preservation}; arguments are omitted here to save space. These identity plots visualize changes in CE magnitude rather than constituting additional rank-preservation tests. Each translucent point represents one concept in one question. The dashed line denotes unchanged CE magnitude; darker regions indicate overlapping observations.
+Figures~\ref{fig:app-rq3-bbq-gptoss} to~\ref{fig:app-rq3-credit-mistral} complement the rank-correlation analysis of Section~\ref{sec:results-ranking-preservation}. Each translucent point is one concept in one question, with the raw Shapley CE on the horizontal axis and the adjusted Shapley CE after normalization on the vertical axis. The two variables are defined in Section~\ref{sec:methodology-preservation}, and their arguments are omitted here to save space. The dashed line denotes unchanged CE magnitude, and darker regions indicate overlapping observations. These identity plots visualize changes in CE magnitude rather than constituting additional rank-preservation tests. Each caption reports the number of concept-level values and the aggregate Spearman correlation $\rho$ from Box~\ref{hyp:hyp3-results}.
 
-Figure~\ref{fig:appendix-rq3-credit-concept-delta} summarizes the New German Credit adjustments by concept. For each of the 12 shared concepts, $\Delta\mathrm{CE}=\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}-\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{raw}}$ is averaged across the 30 evaluated questions. Larger bars identify concepts whose assigned causal effect increases more strongly under the adjusted Shapley normalization. This aggregation identifies concepts that are affected disproportionately on average.

+    {RQ3, BBQ with GPT-OSS 120B: raw and adjusted CE}%
+    {Raw and adjusted concept-level CE for BBQ with GPT-OSS 120B (137 concept-level values, Spearman $\rho=0.937$).}%
+    {fig:app-rq3-bbq-gptoss}%
+  \hfill
+  \ResultFigureHalf{%
+    \NormalizationIdentityPlot{data/appendix/rq3_normalization_identity/bbq_mistral.csv}{-0.3}{1.1}}%
+    {RQ3, BBQ with Mistral Medium 3.5: raw and adjusted CE}%
+    {Raw and adjusted concept-level CE for BBQ with Mistral Medium 3.5 (137 concept-level values, Spearman $\rho=0.903$).}%
+    {fig:app-rq3-bbq-mistral}%
+\end{figure}

-\chapter{Examples}
-\label{app:examples}
-This appendix collects worked examples that complement the formal development of FELICS.
-
-\section*{Effects of Normalization on Pearson Correlation}
-\begin{example_box}[label=ex:payout_normalization_correlation]
-    \textbf{Demonstration:} Payout normalization can alter the Pearson correlation between CE and EE vectors.
-
-    \smallskip
-    \textbf{Setup:} Consider five concepts evaluated using the original metric of Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} and using FELICS. The underlying answer-distribution changes are held fixed. To isolate the statistical consequence of normalization, the adjusted values are assumed directly rather than derived from particular joint interventions, reference sets, or payout coefficients. Suppose the two methods produce
-    \begin{align}
-        \mathbf{CE}_{\mathrm{Matton}} & = \left[1,\,1,\,1,\,1,\,2\right], \\
-        \mathbf{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}    & = \left[1.1,\,0.8,\,0.9,\,0.8,\,2.4\right].
-    \end{align}
-    Both vectors have the same total causal-effect mass and therefore the same mean:
-    \begin{equation}
-        \sum_{i=1}^{5}\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{Matton},i}
-        =\sum_{i=1}^{5}\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj},i}=6,
-        \qquad
-        \mu_{\mathbf{CE}_{\mathrm{Matton}}}
-        =\mu_{\mathbf{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}}=1.2.
-    \end{equation}
-
-    \smallskip
-    \textbf{Explanation-Implied Effects:} Assume the LLM’s explanations yield
-    \begin{equation}
-        \mathbf{EE} = \left[1.0,\, 0.5,\, 1.0,\, 1.0,\, 2.0\right].
-    \end{equation}
-
-    \smallskip
-    \textbf{Pearson Correlation Computation:} The explanation-effect vector has mean $\mu_{\mathbf{EE}}=1.1$. For the original metric,
-    \begin{align}
-        \operatorname{cov}(\mathbf{CE}_{\mathrm{Matton}}, \mathbf{EE}) & = \frac{1}{5}\sum_{i=1}^5 (\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{Matton},i} - 1.2)(\mathrm{EE}_i - 1.1) = 0.18, \\
-        \operatorname{var}(\mathbf{CE}_{\mathrm{Matton}})              & = \frac{1}{5}\sum_{i=1}^5 (\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{Matton},i} - 1.2)^2 = 0.16, \\
-        \operatorname{var}(\mathbf{EE})                                 & = \frac{1}{5}\sum_{i=1}^5 (\mathrm{EE}_i - 1.1)^2 = 0.24, \\
-        \mathrm{PCC}_{\mathrm{Matton}}                                  & = \frac{0.18}{\sqrt{0.16 \cdot 0.24}} \approx 0.919.
-    \end{align}
-    For the assumed normalized FELICS vector,
-    \begin{align}
-        \operatorname{cov}(\mathbf{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}, \mathbf{EE}) & = \frac{1}{5}\sum_{i=1}^5 (\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj},i} - 1.2)(\mathrm{EE}_i - 1.1) = 0.28, \\
-        \operatorname{var}(\mathbf{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}})              & = \frac{1}{5}\sum_{i=1}^5 (\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj},i} - 1.2)^2 = 0.372, \\
-        \mathrm{PCC}_{\mathrm{adj}}                                 & = \frac{0.28}{\sqrt{0.372 \cdot 0.24}} \approx 0.937.
-    \end{align}
-
-    \smallskip
-    \textbf{Conclusion:} Although normalization preserves the sum and mean of the CE values in this example, it changes how the values are distributed around that mean. Consequently, both the CE variance and its covariance with $\mathbf{EE}$ change, yielding $\mathrm{PCC}_{\mathrm{adj}}\approx0.937$ instead of $\mathrm{PCC}_{\mathrm{Matton}}\approx0.919$. Equality of the aggregate CE score therefore does not imply equality of the resulting Pearson faithfulness score.
-\end{example_box}
-
-\section*{Softmax-Ceiling in FELICS in Practice}
-\begin{example_box}[label=ex:softmax_ceiling]
-    \textbf{Scenario:} Let the prediction space consist of two outcomes, $\mathcal{Y} = \{y_1, y_2\}$. Suppose the model's baseline internal scores (logits) before any intervention are $\theta_{y_1} = \ln(4)$ and $\theta_{y_2} = \ln(6)$.
-
-    Using the softmax formula (Equation~\ref{eq:culprit_softmax}), the baseline probability for $y_1$ is:
-    \begin{equation} P(y_1) = \frac{e^{\ln(4)}}{e^{\ln(4)} + e^{\ln(6)}} = \frac{4}{4 + 6} = 0.40 \quad (40\%) \end{equation}
-
-    \smallskip
-    \textbf{Individual Interventions:}
-    Assume that intervening on a concept acts additively in the model's logit space.
-    \begin{itemize}
-        \item \textbf{Concept A:} Increases the logit of $y_1$ by $\Delta_A = \ln(2.25)$.
-              \begin{equation} P^{(A)}(y_1) = \frac{4 \cdot e^{\ln(2.25)}}{(4 \cdot e^{\ln(2.25)}) + 6} = \frac{4 \cdot 2.25}{9 + 6} = \frac{9}{15} = 0.60 \quad (60\%) \end{equation}
-              \emph{Individual effect of A:} $60\% - 40\% = \mathbf{+20\%}$
-
-        \item \textbf{Concept B:} Independently increases the logit of $y_1$ by $\Delta_B = \ln(3.5)$.
-              \begin{equation} P^{(B)}(y_1) = \frac{4 \cdot e^{\ln(3.5)}}{(4 \cdot e^{\ln(3.5)}) + 6} = \frac{4 \cdot 3.5}{14 + 6} = \frac{14}{20} = 0.70 \quad (70\%) \end{equation}
-              \emph{Individual effect of B:} $70\% - 40\% = \mathbf{+30\%}$
-    \end{itemize}
-
-    \smallskip
-    \textbf{Naive Expected Joint Effect:} If probabilities were perfectly additive, intervening on A and B together would shift the probability by $20\% + 30\% = \mathbf{+50\%}$, yielding a final probability of $90\%$.
-
-    \smallskip
-    \textbf{Actual Joint Intervention (The Soft Ceiling):}
-    When intervening on both concepts, their logit changes combine additively ($\Delta_A + \Delta_B = \ln(2.25) + \ln(3.5) = \ln(7.875)$). The softmax function computes the new joint probability as:
-    \begin{equation} P^{(A,B)}(y_1) = \frac{4 \cdot e^{\ln(7.875)}}{(4 \cdot e^{\ln(7.875)}) + 6} = \frac{4 \cdot 7.875}{31.5 + 6} = \frac{31.5}{37.5} = 0.84 \quad (84\%) \end{equation}
-
-    \smallskip
-    \emph{Actual joint effect:} $84\% - 40\% = \mathbf{+44\%}$
-
-    \smallskip
-    \textbf{Conclusion:} Due to the growing denominator in the softmax function, the actual joint effect ($+44\%$) falls short of the naive additive sum ($+50\%$).
-\end{example_box}

-\chapter{Mathematical Demonstrations}
-\label{app:mathematical_demonstrations}
-This appendix provides supporting derivations for properties of FELICS referenced in the main text. The demonstrations address the singleton special case, compatibility under reference-relative additivity, the intended preservation of the dataset-average causal effect, and the two-concept specialization used to compare adjusted and atomic effects.
-
-\section*{Equality of Causal Effect between FELICS singleton-groups and Original Metric}
-\begin{math_box}[label=ma:one_item_group]
-    Let the candidate interaction group consist of exactly one concept,
-    \begin{equation}
-        s=\{C_m\}.
-    \end{equation}
-
-    The payout coefficient introduced in Section~\ref{sec:shapley_correction} becomes
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        \gamma(\mathbf{x},s)
-        =
-        \frac{v_{\mathbf{x}}(s)}
-        {\sum_{C_i\in s}v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_i\})}
-        =
-        \frac{v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_m\})}
-        {v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_m\})}
-        =1.
-    \end{equation}
-
-    Since the reference set used to compute the correction factor
-    $\bar\gamma(\mathbf{x},s,T)$ is restricted to sets of identical treatment size,
-    the only admissible reference size is likewise $|T|=1$. Consequently,
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        \bar\gamma(\mathbf{x},s,T)=1,
-    \end{equation}
-
-    and the normalized value function reduces to
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(T)
-        =
-        \frac{v_{\mathbf{x}}(T)}
-        {\bar\gamma(\mathbf{x},s,T)}
-        =
-        v_{\mathbf{x}}(T).
-    \end{equation}
-
-    The adjusted causal effect is then obtained from the Shapley formulation
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        \mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}(\mathbf{x},C_m,s)
-        =
-        \sum_{T\subseteq s\setminus\{C_m\}}
-        \frac{|T|!(|s|-|T|-1)!}{|s|!}
-        \left[
-            v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(T\cup\{C_m\})
-            -
-            v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(T)
-            \right].
-    \end{equation}
-
-    Since $s=\{C_m\}$, the only admissible coalition is the empty set,
-    $T=\emptyset$. The weighting coefficient therefore simplifies to
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        \frac{0!(1-0-1)!}{1!}=1,
-    \end{equation}
-
-    and using the convention $v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(\emptyset)=0$,
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        \mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}(\mathbf{x},C_m,s)
-        =
-        v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(\{C_m\}).
-    \end{equation}
-
-    Because no normalization is applied for singleton groups,
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(\{C_m\})
-        =
-        v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_m\}),
-    \end{equation}
-
-    which is exactly the value function underlying the causal-effect definition of Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}. Therefore,
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        \mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}(\mathbf{x},C_m,\{C_m\})
-        =
-        \mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{Matton}}(\mathbf{x},C_m)
-    \end{equation}
-
-    showing that FELICS reduces exactly to the original CE when every candidate interaction group contains only a single concept. Because the definitions of the explanation-implied effect and faithfulness (see Equations~\ref{eq:ee} and \ref{eq:faithfulness}) are unchanged, both metrics are equivalent in this scenario.
-\end{math_box}
-
-\section*{Two-Concept Difference from the Atomic Effect}
-\begin{math_box}[label=ma:two_concept_ce_difference]
-    Consider a two-concept candidate interaction group
-    $s=\{C_i,C_j\}$ and abbreviate the singleton values by
-    \begin{equation}
-        a:=v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_i\}),
-        \qquad
-        b:=v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_j\}).
-    \end{equation}
-    Singleton payout coefficients and their same-sized reference coefficients are
-    one. Hence, the adjusted singleton values remain $a$ and $b$. For the
-    two-concept coalition, write
-    $\bar\gamma_s:=\bar\gamma(\mathbf{x},s,s)$. Its adjusted value is
-    \begin{equation}
-        v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(s)
-        =
-        \frac{v_{\mathbf{x}}(s)}{\bar\gamma_s}
-        =
-        \frac{\gamma(\mathbf{x},s)(a+b)}{\bar\gamma_s},
-    \end{equation}
-    where the second equality follows from the definition of the payout
-    coefficient.
-
-    For $C_i$, the two possible predecessor coalitions in the Shapley
-    calculation are $\emptyset$ and $\{C_j\}$, each with weight one half.
-    With $v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(\emptyset)=0$, this yields
-    \begin{align}
-        \mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}(\mathbf{x},C_i,s)
-        & = \frac{1}{2}
-        \left[
-        v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(\{C_i\})
-        -v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(\emptyset)
-        \right]
-        + \frac{1}{2}
-        \left[
-        v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(s)
-        -v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(\{C_j\})
-        \right] \\
-        & = \frac{1}{2}
-        \left[
-        a + \frac{\gamma(\mathbf{x},s)(a+b)}{\bar\gamma_s} - b
-        \right].
-    \end{align}
-    The atomic estimate for $C_i$ is
-    $\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{Matton}}(\mathbf{x},C_i)=a$. Subtracting it from
-    the adjusted Shapley value gives
-    \begin{equation}
-        \mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}(\mathbf{x},C_i,s)
-        - \mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{Matton}}(\mathbf{x},C_i)
-        =
-        \frac{a+b}{2}
-        \left(
-        \frac{\gamma(\mathbf{x},s)}{\bar\gamma_s}-1
-        \right).
-    \end{equation}
-    The same calculation applies symmetrically to $C_j$. This specialization
-    explains why the two-concept difference can be expressed through the
-    coalition payout coefficient and its reference level; for larger groups,
-    the individual Shapley marginal contributions must be evaluated explicitly.
-\end{math_box}
-
-\section*{Compatibility under Reference-Relative Additivity}
-\begin{math_box}[label=ma:independence_behavior]
-    Assume a candidate interaction group $s$ that satisfies \emph{reference-relative
-    additivity}: for every non-empty coalition $T\subseteq s$, its payout
-    coefficient equals the singleton-weighted reference coefficient for treatments
-    of the same size,
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        \bar\gamma(\mathbf{x},s,T)=\gamma(\mathbf{x},T)>\varepsilon,
-        \qquad \forall\,\emptyset\neq T\subseteq s.
-    \end{equation}
-
-    This is a sufficient condition for the reference-relative additivity
-    introduced in Section~\ref{sec:shapley_correction}; it is distinct from
-    absolute additivity, which would require $\gamma(\mathbf{x},T)=1$. Under this
-    assumption, the adjusted value of any non-empty coalition becomes
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(T)
-        =
-        \frac{v_{\mathbf{x}}(T)}
-        {\gamma(\mathbf{x},T)}.
-    \end{equation}
-
-    Substituting the definition of the payout coefficient,
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        \gamma(\mathbf{x},T)
-        =
-        \frac{v_{\mathbf{x}}(T)}
-        {\sum_{C_i\in T}v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_i\})},
-    \end{equation}
-
-    yields
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(T)
-        =
-        \sum_{C_i\in T}v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_i\}).
-    \end{equation}
-
-    Hence, after normalization, every coalition value equals the sum of its
-    members' singleton intervention effects. The marginal contribution of a concept
-    is therefore identical in every coalition in which it appears,
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(T\cup\{C_m\})
-        -
-        v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(T)
-        =
-        v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_m\}),
-        \qquad
-        \forall\,T\subseteq s\setminus\{C_m\}.
-    \end{equation}
-
-    Since the Shapley weights form a partition of unity,
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        \sum_{T\subseteq s\setminus\{C_m\}}
-        \frac{|T|!(|s|-|T|-1)!}{|s|!}
-        =
-        1,
-    \end{equation}
-
-    the adjusted causal effect reduces to
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        \mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}(\mathbf{x},C_m,s)
-        =
-        v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_m\}).
-    \end{equation}
-
-    Finally, $v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_m\})$ is precisely the KL-divergence-based intervention value
-    used by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} to define the causal
-    effect of an individual concept (see Equation~\ref{eq:ce}). Consequently,
-
-    \begin{equation}
-        \mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}(\mathbf{x},C_m,s)
-        =
-        \mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{Matton}}(\mathbf{x},C_m),
-    \end{equation}
-
-    showing compatibility with the original metric for groups satisfying the stated
-    reference-relative additivity assumptions.
-\end{math_box}

-\section*{CE Dataset-Average Preservation}
-\begin{math_box}[label=ma:dataset_average_preservation]
-    \textbf{Claim:} The singleton-weighted reference coefficient, formed as a
-    ratio of aggregate joint effects to aggregate singleton effects, preserves the dataset-level average Causal Effect
-    exactly when it is computed over the complete population of equally sized
-    candidate interaction groups. A finite, target-excluding reference set provides an approximate
-    estimator of this coefficient under the assumptions stated below. Consequently,
-    the FELICS average is expected to approximate that of Matton et
-    al.'s~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} original metric:
-    \begin{equation}
-        \frac{1}{N_C}
-        \sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{X}}
-        \sum_{s\in\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{x})}
-        \sum_{C_m\in s}
-        \mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}(\mathbf{x},C_m,s)
-        \approx
-        \frac{1}{N_C}
-        \sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{X}}
-        \sum_{C_m\in\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{x})}
-        v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_m\}).
-    \end{equation}
-    Here, $N_C$ is the total number of concepts, $\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{x})$
-    is the question-specific concept set, and $\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{x})$ is its
-    partition into candidate interaction groups.
-
-    \smallskip
-    \textbf{Aggregate-Preserving Population Coefficient:} For a fixed treatment
-    size $k$, define the population
-    \begin{equation}
-        \mathcal{G}_k
-        :=
-        \{(\mathbf{x},s)\mid \mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{X},\,
-        s\in\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{x}),\,|s|=k\}.
-    \end{equation}
-    For each $q=(\mathbf{x},s)\in\mathcal{G}_k$, let
-    \begin{equation}
-        a_q:=v_{\mathbf{x}}(s),
-        \qquad
-        b_q:=\sum_{C_m\in s}v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_m\}),
-        \qquad
-        \gamma_q:=\frac{a_q}{b_q},
-    \end{equation}
-    where $b_q>0$. The demonstration concerns non-degenerate group sizes $k$ for
-    which the coefficient defined below also satisfies $g_k>\varepsilon$. The
-    size-specific coefficient that preserves the aggregate is
-    \begin{equation}
-        \label{eq:aggregate_preserving_gamma}
-        g_k
-        :=
-        \frac{\sum_{q\in\mathcal{G}_k}a_q}
-             {\sum_{q\in\mathcal{G}_k}b_q}
-        =
-        \frac{\sum_{q\in\mathcal{G}_k}b_q\gamma_q}
-             {\sum_{q\in\mathcal{G}_k}b_q}.
-    \end{equation}
-    Thus, $g_k$ is a singleton-effect-weighted average of the payout
-    coefficients, rather than their ordinary arithmetic mean.
-
-    \smallskip
-    \textbf{Exact Preservation for the Population Coefficient:} Suppose that
-    every group $s$ of size $k$ is normalized using $g_k$. By Shapley efficiency,
-    \begin{equation}
-        \label{eq:shapley_efficiency_aggregate}
-        \sum_{C_m\in s}
-        \mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}(\mathbf{x},C_m,s)
-        =v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(s)
-        =\frac{v_{\mathbf{x}}(s)}{g_k}.
-    \end{equation}
-    Equation~\ref{eq:shapley_efficiency_aggregate} applies to every
-    $(\mathbf{x},s)\in\mathcal{G}_k$. Summing both sides over this population
-    therefore yields
-    \begin{equation}
-        \label{eq:aggregate_shapley_sum}
-        \begin{aligned}
-            &\sum_{(\mathbf{x},s)\in\mathcal{G}_k}
-            \left[
-                \sum_{C_m\in s}
-                \mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}(\mathbf{x},C_m,s)
-            \right] \\
-            &\qquad=
-            \sum_{(\mathbf{x},s)\in\mathcal{G}_k}
-            \frac{v_{\mathbf{x}}(s)}{g_k}
-            =
-            \frac{1}{g_k}
-            \sum_{(\mathbf{x},s)\in\mathcal{G}_k}v_{\mathbf{x}}(s).
-        \end{aligned}
-    \end{equation}
-    The last equality is valid because $g_k$ depends only on the common group
-    size $k$ and is consequently constant with respect to the summation over
-    $(\mathbf{x},s)$.
-
-    To make the substitution from
-    Equation~\ref{eq:aggregate_preserving_gamma} explicit, abbreviate the
-    aggregate joint- and singleton-effect masses by
-    \begin{equation*}
-        A_k
-        :=\sum_{(\mathbf{x},s)\in\mathcal{G}_k}v_{\mathbf{x}}(s),
-        \qquad
-        B_k
-        :=\sum_{(\mathbf{x},s)\in\mathcal{G}_k}
-        \sum_{C_m\in s}v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_m\}).
-    \end{equation*}
-    Equation~\ref{eq:aggregate_preserving_gamma} then states
-    $g_k=A_k/B_k$. Since the non-degeneracy assumptions give $A_k>0$ and
-    $B_k>0$, the right-hand side of
-    Equation~\ref{eq:aggregate_shapley_sum} becomes
-    \begin{equation}
-        \label{eq:aggregate_preservation_size_k}
-        \frac{1}{g_k}A_k
-        =\frac{1}{A_k/B_k}A_k
-        =\frac{B_k}{A_k}A_k
-        =B_k
-        =\sum_{(\mathbf{x},s)\in\mathcal{G}_k}
-        \sum_{C_m\in s}v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_m\}).
-    \end{equation}
-    Combining Equations~\ref{eq:aggregate_shapley_sum} and
-    \ref{eq:aggregate_preservation_size_k} proves exact aggregate preservation
-    for groups of size $k$.
-
-    Since $\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{x})$ partitions $\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{x})$,
-    summing this identity over all treatment sizes and dividing by $N_C$ yields
-    exact equality of the two dataset averages.
-
-    \smallskip
-    \textbf{Singleton-Weighted Reference Coefficient:} The finite-reference
-    version of the coefficient is the following ratio of aggregates:
-    \begin{equation}
-        \label{eq:aggregate_reference_estimator}
-        \bar\gamma(\mathbf{x},s,T)
-        :=
-        \frac{
-            \displaystyle
-            \sum_{(\mathbf{x}'',T'')\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{x},s,T)}
-            v_{\mathbf{x}''}(T'')
-        }{
-            \displaystyle
-            \sum_{(\mathbf{x}'',T'')\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{x},s,T)}
-            \sum_{C_i\in T''}v_{\mathbf{x}''}(\{C_i\})
-        }.
-    \end{equation}
-    For non-degenerate references, this is equivalent to weighting each payout
-    coefficient by the corresponding treatment's aggregate singleton effect.
-
-    \smallskip
-    \textbf{Assumptions Made:}
-    \begin{enumerate}
-        \item For a target of size
-        $k$, the reference set is representative of $\mathcal{G}_k$ with respect
-        to both the joint effects $a_q$ and singleton-effect totals $b_q$.
-
-        \item The aggregate singleton-effect
-        denominator in Equation~\ref{eq:aggregate_reference_estimator} is positive
-        and sufficiently far from zero, and the resulting coefficient exceeds
-        $\varepsilon$ from Section~\ref{sec:corrected_value_function}. Degenerate size groups and reference sets invoke the
-        unadjusted-value fallback and lie outside the preservation claim.
-
-        \item The selected references provide enough
-        coverage for their aggregate ratio to approximate the complete-population
-        ratio $g_k$.
-    \end{enumerate}
-    Under these conditions,
-    $\bar\gamma(\mathbf{x},s,s)\approx g_{|s|}$. Replacing $g_{|s|}$ in
-    Equation~\ref{eq:shapley_efficiency_aggregate} with this target-specific
-    estimate turns the exact population identity into an approximation.
-\end{math_box}

 \section*{Background}
-In order to illustrate the how unfaithfulness can manifest itself in practice, a special scenario was designed where the LLM was expected to be unfaithful. Past research has shown that most mainstream-LLM tend be leaning left of the political spectrum \cite{rozadoPoliticalPreferencesLLMs2024}. Those results also have been replicated for the party landscape in Germany by Rettenberger \cite{rettenbergerAssessingPoliticalBias2025}, where on average high agreement rates with the left-center \enquote{DIE GRÜNEN} (the greens) where scored, while the very-influential party \enquote{CDU}, which is considered conservative, had below-average results.
+In order to illustrate how unfaithfulness can manifest itself in practice, a special scenario was designed where the LLM was expected to be unfaithful. Past research has shown that most mainstream LLMs tend to lean to the left of the political spectrum \cite{rozadoPoliticalPreferencesLLMs2024}. Those results also have been replicated for the party landscape in Germany by Rettenberger \cite{rettenbergerAssessingPoliticalBias2025}, where on average high agreement rates with the left-center \enquote{DIE GRÜNEN} (the Greens) were scored, while the very influential party \enquote{CDU}, which is considered conservative, had below-average results.
 
 \section*{Basic Setup}
-Building on this proven premise, the LLM was tasked with making a recommendation in favor of exactly one of two candidates for a fictional general national election \enquote{based on which candidate would, according to the information provided, be expected to benefit voters in Germany the most}. Both candidates are purely fictional. Candidate B was designed in a way to come off as the more experienced politician, where as Candidate A seems to be more a newcomer to national politics. Therefore, its arguable that Candidate B has the better profile when political aspects are set aside, making the decision on one of the candidates not fully random and equally distributed once \textit{Party} (and \textit{Political Priorities} in the second run) are removed, which is better for demonstration. The prompt with which the LLM was qued with can be found in in Part \ref{app:prompts} of this appendix.

 \section*{Explanation Extraction}
-Causal Concept Faithfulness by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} and FELICS both extract the concepts described as influential for decision from the natural language explanation via an auxiliary LLM. However to reduce the effort in setting up this simple experiment, the LLM to evaluate itself was asked to provide a list of the influential concepts in conjunction with the explanation, making it possible to parse it directly from the response. The alignment of both the list and the explanation where verified by human reviewers.
+Causal Concept Faithfulness by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} and FELICS both extract the concepts described as influential for the decision from the natural language explanation via an auxiliary LLM. However, to reduce the effort in setting up this simple experiment, the evaluated LLM itself was asked to provide a list of the influential concepts in conjunction with the explanation, making it possible to parse it directly from the response. The alignment of the list and the explanation was verified by human reviewers.

 \section*{Second Run}
-Beyond showing the appearance of unfaithful explanations, the experiment was modified by introducting \textit{Political Priorities}. Both candidates where assigned a short list of main points such as \enquote{Climate Preservation} for the left-center and \enquote{Tax Cuts} for the center-right candidate which closely align with the main ideologies of the two parties. By doing so, a practical example was crafted where intervening on one concept alone is not sufficient to measure its effect because a second still present concept is hinting at it.
+Beyond showing the appearance of unfaithful explanations, the experiment was modified by introducing \textit{Political Priorities}. Both candidates were assigned a short list of main points such as \enquote{Climate Preservation} for the left-center and \enquote{Tax Cuts} for the center-right candidate which closely align with the main ideologies of the two parties. By doing so, a practical example was crafted where intervening on one concept alone is not sufficient to measure its effect because a second still present concept is hinting at it.
 
-To illustrate that \textit{Party} still contributes significant to the prediction even though it causes barely any shift when removed alone, both concepts where removed simultaniously in the fourth tested combination.
+To illustrate that \textit{Party} still contributes significantly to the prediction even though it causes barely any shift when removed alone, both concepts were removed simultaneously in the fourth tested combination.
 
 \section*{Key Findings and Interpretation}
-As shown in Chapter~\ref{ch:introduction}, the LLM showed moderate unfaithfulness in the first example, which was proven by removing the \textit{Party} aspect from both candidates profile and swapping the affiliation in another run. The further major reduction in decisions in favor of Candidate A once parties where reintroduced but flipped (from 12\% to 3\%) illustrates even more the degree the model dislikes the \enquote{CDU} party.
+As shown in Chapter~\ref{ch:introduction}, the LLM showed moderate unfaithfulness in the first example, which was proven by removing the \textit{Party} aspect from both candidates' profiles and swapping the affiliation in another run. The further major reduction in decisions in favor of Candidate A once parties were reintroduced but flipped (from 12\% to 3\%) illustrates even more the degree to which the model dislikes the \enquote{CDU} party.
 
 A further analysis on the samples collected for the original showed that \textit{Party} was mentioned in explanations much more when the left-leaning Candidate A was actually selected. This could also hint at the LLM not actually listing the decisive factors in this setup but post-hoc justifying the decision. Rerunning the experiment with a slightly modified prompt is needed to evaluate the scale of this phenomenon.

 For each pair of normalized categories, the co-grouping rate is the proportion of concept pairs that the oracle assigned to the same group. It is calculated by dividing the number of pairs assigned to the same interaction candidate group by the total number of pairs with those categories. Only distinct concepts from the same question are paired, and the counts are pooled across runs before calculating the rate. Diagonal cells apply the same calculation to pairs of concepts within a single category. If no eligible pairs exist, the cell is undefined and shown in gray. The use of conditional percentages prevents frequent categories from dominating solely because they occur more often.

@@ -5,15 +5,15 @@ This chapter establishes the conceptual and methodological background required f
 This section introduces explainability in the context of large language models. It outlines the purpose of explanations, the regulatory and stakeholder demands placed on them, the principal dimensions by which they can be assessed, and the challenges that arise specifically for LLMs.
 
 \subsection{Definition and Purpose}
-Large language models have seen increasing adoption in recent years for tasks such as question answering \cite{allemangIncreasingAccuracyLLM2025}, writing \cite{liangWidespreadAdoptionLarge2025}, and software development \cite{zhangSurveyLargeLanguage2026}, but also in high-stakes domains such as healthcare \cite{maityLargeLanguageModels2025, laiLargeLanguageModels2024, hungWalkingTightropeEvaluating2023}. However, since LLMs are structurally complex deep learning models with millions of neurons and billions of parameters, they are often considered black boxes \cite{qamarUnderstandingBlackboxInterpretable2023}. At such scale, calculating or mapping interactions among components is often practically infeasible. This makes it challenging to comprehend and evaluate their decisions
+Large language models have seen increasing adoption in recent years for tasks such as question answering \cite{allemangIncreasingAccuracyLLM2025}, writing \cite{liangWidespreadAdoptionLarge2025}, and software development \cite{zhangSurveyLargeLanguage2026}, but also in high-stakes domains such as healthcare \cite{maityLargeLanguageModels2025, laiLargeLanguageModels2024, hungWalkingTightropeEvaluating2023}. However, since LLMs are structurally complex deep learning models with millions of neurons and billions of parameters, they are often considered black boxes \cite{qamarUnderstandingBlackboxInterpretable2023}. At such scale, calculating or mapping interactions among components is often computationally expensive. This makes it challenging to comprehend and evaluate decisions.
 
-\emph{Explainable AI} (XAI) is a research field that seeks to make artificial intelligence algorithms transparent and understandable \cite{hsiehComprehensiveGuideExplainable2024}, thereby making the inherent black box less opaque. Central to this research field is the concept of explainability, whose precise definition remains disputed \cite{roscherExplainableMachineLearning2020}. The term is described in the relevant ISO standard as the \enquote{level of understanding of how the AI-based system came up with a given result} \cite{ISOIECTR}. Explainability aims to justify decisions and demonstrate that they are correct and unbiased in order to build trust with users \cite{aliExplainableArtificialIntelligence2023}. The term \emph{interpretability} is often used in a similar context, although there is a subtle distinction between the two concepts. Interpretability concerns understanding the inner workings of an AI model, whereas explainability focuses on making the decision process retraceable \cite{garouaniInvestigatingDualityInterpretability2024, aliExplainableArtificialIntelligence2023}. For example, a decision tree exposes its structure and is therefore interpretable. A complex neural network showing a heatmap on an image classification task might be explainable without being interpretable.
+\emph{Explainable AI} (XAI) is a research field that seeks to make artificial intelligence algorithms transparent and understandable \cite{hsiehComprehensiveGuideExplainable2024}, thereby making the inherent black box less opaque. Central to this research field is the concept of \emph{explainability}, whose precise definition remains disputed \cite{roscherExplainableMachineLearning2020}. The term \emph{interpretability} is often used in a similar context, although there is a subtle distinction between the two concepts. Interpretability concerns understanding the inner workings of an AI model, whereas explainability focuses on making the decision process retraceable \cite{garouaniInvestigatingDualityInterpretability2024, aliExplainableArtificialIntelligence2023}. For example, a decision tree exposes its structure and is therefore interpretable. A complex neural network showing a heat map on an image classification task might be explainable without being interpretable.
 \subsection{Transparency as Regulatory Requirement}
 \label{sec:transparency_as_regulatory_requirement}
 The recent increase in the importance of XAI research has also been driven by new regulatory measures. While multiple countries have introduced their own legislative frameworks to govern the use of AI, such as China \cite{zouNavigatingChinasRegulatory2025} and the United States \cite{BlueprintAIBill}, the European Union's AI Act \cite{RegulationEU20242024} can be regarded as particularly relevant to explainability. Under this regulation, individuals affected by high-risk AI decisions have the right to obtain clear and meaningful explanations regarding the AI system’s role and the main contributing factors to the decision. Furthermore, high-risk AI systems must provide transparency and allow for human oversight \cite{RegulationEU20242024}. The \emph{Right to Explanation} derived from the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) is also relevant to AI systems within the European Union \cite{nisevicExplainableAICan2024}.
 
 \subsection{Audience Groups}
-Providing explanations for reasoning processes or decisions made by an AI system can increase trust in the system among end users and experts \cite{viloneNotionsExplainabilityEvaluation2021, herreraMakingSenseUnsensible2025}, while also assisting researchers and developers in debugging and fine-tuning the model \cite{szymanskiDisentanglingStakeholderRole2025}. Moreover, as argued in Chapter \ref{sec:transparency_as_regulatory_requirement}, explainability can be required by regulations and auditors, thereby forming another stakeholder group for XAI systems \cite{herreraMakingSenseUnsensible2025, szymanskiDisentanglingStakeholderRole2025}.
+Providing explanations for reasoning processes or decisions made by an AI system can increase trust in the system among end users and experts \cite{viloneNotionsExplainabilityEvaluation2021, herreraMakingSenseUnsensible2025}, while also assisting researchers and developers in debugging and fine-tuning the model \cite{szymanskiDisentanglingStakeholderRole2025}. Moreover, as argued in Section \ref{sec:transparency_as_regulatory_requirement}, explainability can be required by regulations and auditors, thereby forming another stakeholder group for XAI systems \cite{herreraMakingSenseUnsensible2025, szymanskiDisentanglingStakeholderRole2025}.
 
 It has also been noted that different audiences perceive and consume explanations differently \cite{herreraReflectionsAttentivenessEXplainable2025, herreraMakingSenseUnsensible2025}. For instance, developers may expect technical information about the inner workings of the system, whereas end users may benefit more from concise and understandable explanations in natural language. Adapting explanations generated by an AI system to the expectations of the recipient has therefore become a subject of recent research \cite{hoffmanExplainableAIRoles2023, mavrepisXAIAllCan2024}.

 \subsection{LLM Explainability Challenges}
 \label{sec:challenges_in_llm_explainability_research}
-According to Zhao et al.~\cite{zhaoExplainabilityLargeLanguage2024, luoUnderstandingUtilizationSurvey2024}, explainability research for LLMs is difficult because understanding their internal processes becomes infeasible due to their enormous size and complex structure. They write that traditional methods such as gradient-based attribution \cite{sundararajanAxiomaticAttributionDeep2017} and SHAP values \cite{lundbergUnifiedApproachInterpreting2017} are impractical for state-of-the-art LLMs with billions of parameters. This structural complexity also hinders in-depth analysis, debugging, and diagnosis of these models. Furthermore, the opaque black-box nature of LLMs amplifies this problem \cite{qamarUnderstandingBlackboxInterpretable2023}. Many mainstream commercial models are only accessible via APIs \cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, meaning that their model weights are not publicly available to users or researchers.
+According to Zhao et al.~\cite{zhaoExplainabilityLargeLanguage2024, luoUnderstandingUtilizationSurvey2024}, explainability research for LLMs is difficult because understanding their internal processes becomes infeasible due to their enormous size. +Despite these limitations, several technical approaches have been developed to gain insight into the black box and retrace important reasoning pathways within an LLM.  At the same time, since LLMs are specifically designed for natural language processing tasks, they also provide an opportunity to generate explanations directly in natural human language \cite{zhaoExplainabilityLargeLanguage2024}. Most notably, this includes \emph{ad-hoc} explanations where the model generates a justification for its decision after giving the prediction and Chain-of-Thought (CoT) explanations, in which LLMs reconstruct their own reasoning process. Prompting an LLM to generate CoT explanations has furthermore been shown to improve performance on complex reasoning tasks \cite{weiChainofthoughtPromptingElicits2022}.
 
@@ -46,7 +46,7 @@ Among the dimensions of explainability introduced above, faithfulness is central
 \label{sec:definition_faithfulness}
 As discussed in Section~\ref{sec:aspects_and_dimensions}, faithfulness describes the degree to which the explanation generated by an LLM is grounded in the model's actual internal reasoning process \cite{herreraMakingSenseUnsensible2025, jacoviFaithfullyInterpretableNLP2020, ribeiroWhyShouldTrust2016}. A faithful explanation should therefore reflect the model's true decision path rather than merely providing a plausible post-hoc rationalization \cite{herreraMakingSenseUnsensible2025}.
 
-LLMs have the ability to give those explanations in natural language \cite{weiChainofthoughtPromptingElicits2022}. But unlike other explainability methods, the faithfulness of these are not guaranteed  \cite{herreraMakingSenseUnsensible2025, jacoviFaithfullyInterpretableNLP2020}. In particular, an LLM may generate a natural language explanation that sounds convincing to users (high plausibility) while being entirely fabricated and not grounded in the model's actual reasoning process, hiding true decision factors (low faithfulness). Such scenarios, especially in high-risk environments, can pose significant risks and lead to misplaced trust in these systems \cite{agarwalFaithfulnessVsPlausibility2024, madsenAreSelfexplanationsLarge2024, herreraMakingSenseUnsensible2025}.
+LLMs have the ability to give those explanations in natural language \cite{weiChainofthoughtPromptingElicits2022}. But unlike other explainability methods, the faithfulness of these is not guaranteed  \cite{herreraMakingSenseUnsensible2025, jacoviFaithfullyInterpretableNLP2020}. In particular, an LLM may generate a natural language explanation that sounds convincing to users (high plausibility) while being entirely fabricated and not grounded in the model's actual reasoning process, hiding true decision factors (low faithfulness). Such scenarios, especially in high-risk environments, can pose significant risks and lead to misplaced trust in these systems \cite{agarwalFaithfulnessVsPlausibility2024, madsenAreSelfexplanationsLarge2024, herreraMakingSenseUnsensible2025}.
 
 In particular, research by Turpin et al.~\cite{turpinLanguageModelsDont2023} demonstrated that LLMs can systematically conceal internalized biases while providing plausible yet unfaithful justifications. When deliberately influenced to rely on prejudicial reasoning in their experiments, the models often used social biases such as gender or race as primary decision factors, but rarely mentioned them explicitly in their explanations. Instead, they resorted to alternative justifications based on conduct or circumstances. This behavior is especially concerning in applications such as recidivism prediction, where fairness may be undermined \cite{farayolaFairnessAIPredicting2023, jacoviFaithfullyInterpretableNLP2020}. Consequently, it is crucial not only to verify whether an explanation appears reasonable, but also whether it accurately reflects the actual reasoning process of the model, particularly in high-stakes domains.

 \subsubsection{Correlation-Based Metrics}
 To address these limitations, Siegel et al.~\cite{siegelProbabilitiesAlsoMatter2024} proposed the Correlational Counterfactual Test (CCT), which significantly extends the approach of Atanasova et al.~\cite{atanasovaFaithfulnessTestsNatural2023}. At its core, CCT measures faithfulness as the correlation between intervention impact scores and explanation mention scores \cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}.
 
-First, the LLM receives an unmodified question from a dataset. The input is then perturbed and presented to the LLM again, producing prediction probability distributions for both the original and counterfactual versions. The counterfactual generation procedure largely follows the earlier work by Atanasova et al.~\cite{atanasovaFaithfulnessTestsNatural2023}, although an auxiliary LLM is additionally used to filter nonsensical interventions. The Total Variation Distance (TVD) between the original and perturbed prediction distributions is then calculated in order to quantify the impact of the intervention. Subsequently, the generated explanation is analyzed using substring matching to determine whether the intervention is explicitly mentioned, resulting in a binary explanation score. This procedure is repeated across multiple dataset examples, after which the Pearson correlation coefficient between intervention impacts and explanation mentions is computed to obtain the final CCT score.
+First, the LLM receives an unmodified question from a dataset. The input is then perturbed and presented to the LLM again, producing prediction probability distributions for both the original and counterfactual versions. The counterfactual generation procedure largely follows the earlier work by Atanasova et al.~\cite{atanasovaFaithfulnessTestsNatural2023}, although an auxiliary LLM is additionally used to filter nonsensical interventions. The Total Variation Distance (TVD) between the original and perturbed prediction distributions is then calculated in order to quantify the impact of the intervention. Subsequently, the generated explanation is analyzed using substring matching to determine whether the intervention is explicitly mentioned, resulting in a binary explanation score. This procedure is repeated across multiple dataset examples, after which the Pearson correlation coefficient (PCC) between intervention impacts and explanation mentions is computed to obtain the final CCT score.
 
 Since the metric builds directly upon the approach by Atanasova et al.~\cite{atanasovaFaithfulnessTestsNatural2023}, several limitations are inherited, particularly with respect to counterfactual generation. For example, the counterfactuals are restricted to the insertion of individual adjectives or adverbs, limiting their semantic diversity. Furthermore, the metric does not account for cases in which an explanation refers to a synonym or semantically equivalent concept rather than the exact inserted word.
 
 \subsubsection{Output Contribution Metrics}
 \label{sec:cc_shap}
-Parcalabescu and Frank \cite{parcalabescuMeasuringFaithfulnessSelfconsistency2024} argue that many existing metrics measure self-consistency of outputs rather than genuine faithfulness (see Section \ref{sec:applying_definitions}). The authors therefore propose their own self-consistency metric, CC-SHAP, which does not depend on modifying the input. Their approach compares the degree to which input tokens contribute to both the generated answer and the explanation. By measuring the convergence between these contributions using Shapley values, the method produces a continuous score.
+Parcalabescu and Frank \cite{parcalabescuMeasuringFaithfulnessSelfconsistency2024} argue that all existing metrics measure self-consistency of outputs rather than genuine faithfulness when the internal structure of the model is not examined. This will be further elaborated in Section \ref{sec:applying_definitions}. Having this constraint clarified, the authors therefore propose their own self-consistency metric, CC-SHAP, which also only operates on output analysis. Their approach compares the degree to which input tokens contribute to both the generated answer and the explanation. By measuring the convergence between these contributions using Shapley values, the method produces a continuous score.
 
-This approach offers several advantages. It avoids the need for input perturbations, does not require semantic evaluation of explanations, supports both CoT and ad-hoc explanations, reveals patterns of consistency and inconsistency at the token level, and does not require auxiliary AI models during evaluation. However, these benefits come at the cost of considerable computational overhead, which may exceed that of other approaches \cite{parcalabescuMeasuringFaithfulnessSelfconsistency2024}.
+This approach offers several advantages. It does not require semantic evaluation of explanations, supports both CoT and ad-hoc explanations, reveals patterns of consistency and inconsistency at the token level, and does not require auxiliary AI models during evaluation. However, these benefits come at the cost of considerable computational overhead, which may exceed that of other approaches \cite{parcalabescuMeasuringFaithfulnessSelfconsistency2024}.
 
 \subsubsection{CoT Corruption Methods}
 Lanham et al.~\cite{lanhamMeasuringFaithfulnessChainofThought2023} investigated faithfulness by deliberately corrupting CoT reasoning in order to test hypotheses regarding CoT behavior. First, the model is prompted to reason step-by-step about a multiple-choice problem. Given the original CoT, the LLM then produces a final answer. The CoT is subsequently modified through several interventions, including truncation, the insertion of mistakes using an auxiliary model, paraphrasing of earlier reasoning steps followed by regeneration of the remainder, and the insertion of filler tokens. The model is then asked again to provide an answer based on the modified CoT.
@@ -94,7 +94,7 @@ A final approach is presented by Tutek et al.~\cite{tutekMeasuringChainThought20
 
 For a given LLM $\mathcal{M}$ and a test question, the model first generates a CoT explanation. The explanation is then decomposed into individual reasoning steps. For each step, the corresponding knowledge is selectively unlearned from the model parameters, producing a fine-tuned model $\mathcal{M}'$ that is discouraged from predicting the removed reasoning step in the given context. Subsequently, both the original model $\mathcal{M}$ and the modified model $\mathcal{M}'$ are asked the same question again, this time without requesting a CoT explanation, and their predictions are compared.
 
-For the proposed ff-hard metric, which evaluates the faithfulness of the entire CoT, the result is binary. A score of 1 indicates that the models disagree on the most likely answer, suggesting high faithfulness, whereas a score of 0 indicates that the prediction remains unchanged and the explanation likely does not reflect the true reasoning process. One major strength of this approach is that it avoids several pitfalls associated with context perturbation methods, such as the model reconstructing the modified context. However, the authors themselves note that high recall cannot be guaranteed: if unlearning a reasoning step does not alter the prediction, this may indicate failed unlearning, the existence of multiple reasoning paths, or the possibility that the step was not actually used. Furthermore, the FUR approach is not applicable to closed-source API-only models.

One major strength of this approach is that it avoids several pitfalls associated with context perturbation methods, such as the model reconstructing the modified context.  \section{Shapley Values in Explainable AI}
 \label{sec:shapley_fundamentals}
@@ -102,14 +102,14 @@ The preceding review shows that Shapley values are a recurring technique in XAI
 
 Shapley values were introduced by Lloyd Shapley in 1953 \cite{shapley17ValueNPerson1953}. Originating in cooperative game theory, they determine how to fairly distribute a total payout among a set of players based on each player's individual contribution to the coalition. A player's allocation is calculated by evaluating its expected marginal contribution across all possible coalitions that the player could form with the remaining players \cite{shapley17ValueNPerson1953, mitchellSamplingPermutationsShapley2022, parcalabescuMeasuringFaithfulnessSelfconsistency2024}.
 
-Given a value function $v(T)$ defined for any subset $T \subseteq \mathbf{P}$, where $\mathbf{P}$ denotes the set of all players, the value allotted to a player $m \in \mathbf{P}$ is
+Given a value function $v(T)$ defined for every coalition $T \subseteq \mathbf{P}$, where $\mathbf{P}$ denotes the set of all players, the payout allotted to player $m \in \mathbf{P}$ is
 
 \begin{equation}
 	\label{eq:shapley}
 	\phi_m(v) = \sum_{T \subseteq \mathbf{P} \setminus \{m\}} \frac{|T|!\, (|\mathbf{P}|-|T|-1)!}{|\mathbf{P}|!} \big[ v(T \cup \{m\}) - v(T) \big].
 \end{equation}
 
-Here, $\frac{|T|!\, (|\mathbf{P}|-|T|-1)!}{|\mathbf{P}|!}$ weights the coalition, while $v(T \cup \{m\}) - v(T)$ is player $m$'s marginal contribution to it. Shapley proved that this solution is unique in satisfying four core axioms \cite{shapley17ValueNPerson1953, mitchellSamplingPermutationsShapley2022, parcalabescuMeasuringFaithfulnessSelfconsistency2024}:
+Here, $\frac{|T|!\, (|\mathbf{P}|-|T|-1)!}{|\mathbf{P}|!}$ weights the coalition, while $v(T \cup \{m\}) - v(T)$ is player $m$'s marginal contribution to it. The term \emph{grand coalition} refers to the complete set of all players $\mathbf{P}$ working together, yielding the total combined value $v(\mathbf{P})$. Shapley proved that this solution is unique in satisfying four core axioms \cite{shapley17ValueNPerson1953, mitchellSamplingPermutationsShapley2022, parcalabescuMeasuringFaithfulnessSelfconsistency2024}:
 \begin{itemize}
 	\item \textbf{Efficiency}: The sum of the individual values equals the total value created by the grand coalition ($\sum_{i \in \mathbf{P}} \phi_i(v) = v(\mathbf{P})$).
 	\item \textbf{Symmetry}: Identical players who contribute the same amount to every coalition receive identical payouts (if $v(T \cup \{i\}) = v(T \cup \{j\})$ for all $T \subseteq \mathbf{P} \setminus \{i, j\}$, then $\phi_i(v) = \phi_j(v)$).

 \label{sec:applying_definitions}
 The preceding sections introduced several terms whose definitions remain contested and often depend on the context in which they are used \cite{liptonMythosModelInterpretability2018, roscherExplainableMachineLearning2020}. Before presenting the Causal Concept Faithfulness Metric proposed by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, this section therefore establishes how the terms most relevant to the present thesis will be understood throughout the remainder of the work.
 
-Throughout this thesis, \emph{explainability} denotes the capacity of an LLM to provide a target audience with an explanation that enables them to understand why \emph{a particular output}, rather than an alternative, was produced \cite{ISOIECTR, aliExplainableArtificialIntelligence2023, roscherExplainableMachineLearning2020}. \emph{Interpretability}, by contrast, describes the user's resulting ability to understand the system and its decision rationale \cite{garouaniInvestigatingDualityInterpretability2024, aliExplainableArtificialIntelligence2023} and is not the primary focus of this work.
+Throughout this thesis, \emph{explainability} denotes the capacity of an LLM to provide a target audience with an explanation that enables them to understand why a particular output, rather than an alternative, was produced \cite{ISOIECTR, aliExplainableArtificialIntelligence2023, roscherExplainableMachineLearning2020}. \emph{Interpretability}, by contrast, describes the user's resulting ability to understand the system and its decision rationale \cite{garouaniInvestigatingDualityInterpretability2024, aliExplainableArtificialIntelligence2023} and is not the primary focus of this work.
 
 An \emph{explanation} is understood as a human-consumable representation that presents one or more factors as reasons for a model output. It is treated as an artifact produced about a prediction and is not assumed to reproduce the model's internal computational process merely because it was generated by the model itself. The scope of this thesis is limited to explanations that express a sequence of intermediate reasoning steps in natural language \cite{zhaoExplainabilityLargeLanguage2024}, usually English. Such explanations are referred to as Chain-of-Thought explanations \cite{weiChainofthoughtPromptingElicits2022} and are produced by the evaluated model itself shortly before it provides its prediction.
 
 Explanations may possess several desirable qualities, among which faithfulness and plausibility are particularly well established in the literature \cite{herreraMakingSenseUnsensible2025, jacoviFaithfullyInterpretableNLP2020, zhuExplanationEraLarge2024, turpinLanguageModelsDont2023}. In accordance with common usage, \emph{plausibility} denotes the extent to which an explanation appears reasonable and accords with a human evaluator's expectations \cite{zhuExplanationEraLarge2024, jacoviFaithfullyInterpretableNLP2020}. The primary concern of this thesis, however, is the estimation of faithfulness.
 
-Parcalabescu and Frank \cite{parcalabescuMeasuringFaithfulnessSelfconsistency2024}, however, contend that estimating true faithfulness is impossible without examining a model's internal mechanisms, such as its parameter weights. They emphasize that contemporary metrics merely quantify the self-consistency between an explanation and a shifting prediction—a phenomenon potentially decoupled from the internal \enquote{reasoning process} \cite{jacoviFaithfullyInterpretableNLP2020}. Despite this limitation, the majority of evaluation methods continue to claim they assess faithfulness (see Section~\ref{sec:estimation_approaches}). Consequently, the widely accepted standard definition proposed by Jacovi and Goldberg \cite{jacoviFaithfullyInterpretableNLP2020} might be, upon stricter inspection, incompatible with how most current metrics actually operate.
+Parcalabescu and Frank \cite{parcalabescuMeasuringFaithfulnessSelfconsistency2024}, however, contend that estimating true faithfulness is impossible without examining a model's internal mechanisms, such as its parameter weights. They emphasize that contemporary metrics merely quantify the self-consistency between an explanation and a shifting prediction, a phenomenon potentially decoupled from the internal \enquote{reasoning process} \cite{jacoviFaithfullyInterpretableNLP2020}. Despite this limitation, the majority of evaluation methods continue to claim they assess faithfulness (see Section~\ref{sec:estimation_approaches}). Consequently, the widely accepted standard definition proposed by Jacovi and Goldberg \cite{jacoviFaithfullyInterpretableNLP2020} might be, upon stricter inspection, incompatible with how most current metrics actually operate.
 
-Considering this, this thesis will use a definition more inline with the counterfactual edits domain \cite{atanasovaFaithfulnessTestsNatural2023, mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}. \emph{Faithfulness} is understood as the degree of alignment between the importance assigned in an explanation to parts of the input and the actual \emph{causal} influence of those input parts on the model's prediction. More precisely, it denotes the extent to which an explanation attributes a model output to factors that causally influenced that output under a specified intervention scheme. In this thesis, faithfulness is estimated by intervening on the input and observing the resulting changes in the output, rather than through direct access to the model's internal reasoning process. \emph{Causal influence} \cite{pearlCausality2009} accordingly refers to the change in the model's output distribution for a counterfactual input induced by a specified intervention on the part of input whose effect should be estimated.
+Considering this, this thesis will use a definition more in line with the counterfactual edits domain \cite{atanasovaFaithfulnessTestsNatural2023, mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}. \emph{Faithfulness} describes how well an explanation reflects the importance the model assigns to different features. More precisely, it is understood as the degree of alignment between the importance assigned in an explanation to parts of the input and the actual \emph{causal} influence of those input parts on the model's prediction. In this thesis, faithfulness is estimated by intervening on the input and observing the resulting changes in the output, rather than through direct access to the model's internal reasoning process. \emph{Causal influence} \cite{pearlCausality2009} accordingly refers to the change in the model's output distribution for a counterfactual input induced by a specified intervention on the part of the input whose effect should be estimated.
 
 Adopting this causal perspective decouples the evaluation from the model's unobservable inner workings. By shifting the focus to input-output interventions, this framework establishes a theoretically sound boundary that explicitly aligns the definition of faithfulness with the operational capabilities of current empirical evaluation techniques.
 
 \section{Causal Concept Faithfulness Metric}
 \label{sec:causal_concept_metric}
-The preceding review of several contemporary evaluation approaches motivates a closer examination of the metric on which this thesis builds. This chapter provides a detailed technical and mathematical account of its design. Starting with a review of it's fundamental idea, the mathematical aspects of the framework are presented, preserving the notation of the original authors. This is followed by a walkthrough of the evaluation pipeline that produces the final metric. The chapter concludes by discussing the promising potential of the Causal Concept Faithfulness Metric for quantifying the faithfulness of LLM-generated explanations, while also examining its existing limitations. This analysis specifically leads to the challenge of handling correlated concepts in the following chapter, which lays the foundation for the remainder of this thesis.
+The preceding review of several contemporary evaluation approaches motivates a closer examination of the metric on which this thesis builds. This chapter provides a detailed technical and mathematical account of its design. Starting with a review of its fundamental idea, the mathematical aspects of the framework are presented, preserving the notation of the original authors. This is followed by a walkthrough of the evaluation pipeline that produces the final metric. The chapter concludes by discussing the promising potential of the Causal Concept Faithfulness Metric for quantifying the faithfulness of LLM-generated explanations, while also examining its existing limitations. This analysis specifically leads to the challenge of handling correlated concepts in the following chapter, which lays the foundation for the remainder of this thesis.
 
 This presentation is an abridged version of the original paper by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}. The interested reader is referred to the original work for further details.
 
 \subsection{Fundamental Idea}
 Building upon the counterfactual edit methodology pioneered by Atanasova et al.~\cite{atanasovaFaithfulnessTestsNatural2023} and Siegel et al.~\cite{siegelProbabilitiesAlsoMatter2024}, Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} proposed an updated evaluation metric in their work. In contrast to prior token-level approaches, their method, the Causal Concept Faithfulness Metric, operates on higher-level concepts such as \textit{gender}, \textit{race}, or \textit{time of day}. Beyond producing a quantitative score, the metric also exposes semantic patterns linked to unfaithful behavior.
 
-Consistent with the terminology established in Section~\ref{sec:applying_definitions}, the authors define faithfulness as the alignment between the concepts that truly influence a model's prediction and the importance the model assigns to those same concepts within its own explanation. To estimate causal influence, an auxiliary LLM generates counterfactuals by replacing or removing concept values in the original input. Following the correlational approach of Siegel et al.~\cite{siegelProbabilitiesAlsoMatter2024}, prediction distributions are then collected for both original and counterfactual questions to estimate the true causal effect. Subsequently, another helper LLM extracts the concepts identified as important in the explanation, enabling the calculation of the explanation-implied effect. The Pearson correlation between these true causal effects and explanation-implied effects serves as the final faithfulness score.
+Consistent with the terminology established in Section~\ref{sec:applying_definitions}, the authors define faithfulness as the alignment between the concepts that truly influence a model's prediction and the importance the model assigns to those same concepts within its own explanation. To estimate causal influence, an auxiliary LLM $\mathcal{A}$ generates counterfactuals by replacing or removing concept values in the original input. $\mathcal{A}$ need not be the same model as $\mathcal{M}$, and for the reasons discussed in Section~\ref{sec:matton_limitations} it should preferably differ from it. Following the correlational approach of Siegel et al.~\cite{siegelProbabilitiesAlsoMatter2024}, prediction distributions of the LLM under evaluation $\mathcal{M}$ are then collected for both original and counterfactual questions to estimate the true causal effect. Subsequently, helper model $\mathcal{A}$ extracts the concepts identified as important in the explanation, enabling the calculation of the explanation-implied effect. The Pearson correlation coefficient between these true causal effects and explanation-implied effects serves as the final faithfulness score.

+The goal is to evaluate the faithfulness of explanations generated by an opaque large language model under evaluation, denoted by $\mathcal{M}$, for a dataset of questions $\mathbf{X}=\{\mathbf{x}_1,\ldots,\mathbf{x}_N\}$. Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} formulate their metric for so-called \emph{context-based questions}, where each response $\mathbf{r}$ produced by the model consists of both a selected answer $y \in \mathcal{Y}$ from a predefined set of answer options $\mathcal{Y}$ and a natural language explanation $e$, i.e.,
 \begin{equation}
-	r=(y,e).
+	\mathbf{r}=(y,e).
 \end{equation}
 
-Following Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, $Y$ denotes the categorical random variable representing the model's answer, whereas $y$ is a realized answer choice in $\mathcal{Y}$. Accordingly, the model's answer distribution for question $\mathbf{x}$ is denoted by $\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}}(Y\mid \mathbf{x})$.
+The model $\mathcal{M}$ is not assumed to produce the same response every time it receives a fixed input. Following Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, $Y$ takes values $y\in\mathcal{Y}$ for a fixed question $\mathbf{x}$ according to the model’s answer distribution. Collecting $S$ samples from $\mathcal{M}$ on $\mathbf{x}$ approximates the underlying distribution $\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}}(Y\mid \mathbf{x})$.
+
+In practice, $\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}}(Y\mid\mathbf{x})$ is not approximated by the relative answer frequencies, as this formal definition suggests. Instead, it is estimated using a categorical likelihood with partial pooling in a \emph{Bayesian hierarchical model}. This helps stabilize the probability distributions with a lower number of samples $S$.

 \subsection{Evaluation Pipeline}
 \label{sec:matton_evaluation_pipeline}
 The mathematical quantities introduced above are estimated through a multi-stage empirical pipeline. It extracts concepts from each question, creates corresponding counterfactuals, gathers model responses, estimates explanation-implied and causal effects, and finally aggregates their alignment into a faithfulness score.
 
 \subsubsection{Extracting Concepts, Values, and Categories}
 
-For each question $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbf{X}$, the first step of the evaluation pipeline is to identify the set of concepts $\mathbf{C}$ contained in the question. As manually annotating concepts for every question would be prohibitively time-consuming, Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} automate this process using an auxiliary LLM $\mathcal{A}$. It should be emphasized that $\mathcal{A}$ is independent of the LLM under evaluation $\mathcal{M}$ and may therefore be a different model.
+For each question $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbf{X}$, the first step of the evaluation pipeline is to identify the set of concepts $\mathbf{C}$ contained in the question. As manually annotating concepts for every question would be prohibitively time-consuming, Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} automate this process using an auxiliary LLM $\mathcal{A}$.
 
 The extraction procedure is performed in two stages. In the first prompt, $\mathcal{A}$ is instructed to identify all concepts contained in the question and to assign each concept to a semantic category from the dataset-wide category set $\mathbf{K}$. Notably, the possible categories are not provided explicitly; instead, the model is expected to infer appropriate category names consistently across the dataset.
 
-In a second prompt, the auxiliary model extracts the current value $c_m$ of every identified concept $C_m$ together with at least one plausible alternative value $c'_m$. These values define the intervention set $\mathbb{C}_m$ required for subsequent counterfactual generation. To improve quality and robustness, every prompt in the pipeline contains several few-shot examples that guide the auxiliary model towards the desired output format. The extracted information is then parsed and stored by the evaluation framework before the pipeline proceeds to the generation of counterfactual questions.
+In a second prompt, the auxiliary model extracts the current value $c_m$ of every identified concept $C_m$ together with at least one plausible alternative value $c'_m$. These values define the intervention set $\mathbb{C}_m$ required for subsequent counterfactual generation. To improve quality and robustness, every prompt in the pipeline contains several few-shot examples that guide the auxiliary model toward the desired output format. The extracted information is then parsed and stored by the evaluation framework before the pipeline proceeds to the generation of counterfactual questions.
 
 \subsubsection{Generating Counterfactual Questions}
 
 Once the concepts and their corresponding values have been identified, counterfactual questions can be generated. For each concept $C_m$, the original question $\mathbf{x}$, the current concept value $c_m$, and an alternative value $c'_m$ are provided to the auxiliary model $\mathcal{A}$. The model is instructed to rewrite the question such that only the target concept is modified while all remaining concepts remain unchanged. Furthermore, the rewritten question should preserve the original wording and sentence structure as closely as possible in order to minimize unintended linguistic differences that could influence the predictions of $\mathcal{M}$.
 
-Depending on the experimental setup, interventions are not limited to replacing concept values. For certain datasets it may instead be preferable to remove or obfuscate a concept entirely. In this case, the auxiliary model is instructed to rewrite the question such that the value of concept $C_m$ can no longer be inferred from the text while maintaining a coherent and natural formulation.
+Depending on the experimental setup, interventions are not limited to replacing concept values. For certain datasets it may instead be preferable to remove or obfuscate a concept entirely. In this case, the auxiliary model is instructed to rewrite the question such that the value of concept $C_m$ can no longer be inferred from the text while maintaining a coherent formulation.
 
 \subsubsection{Collecting Model Responses}
 \label{sec:collecting_model_responses}
-The next stage of the pipeline consists of collecting responses from the LLM under evaluation $\mathcal{M}$. To estimate the causal concept effects defined in Equation~\ref{eq:ce}, prediction distributions are required for both the original question $\mathbf{x}$ and every generated counterfactual question

 \subsubsection{Estimating Explanation-Implied Effects}
 
 The explanation-implied effects quantify whether the model itself presents a concept as causally relevant within its explanations. To estimate this quantity automatically, every explanation generated by $\mathcal{M}$ is analyzed by the auxiliary model $\mathcal{A}$.
 
-A key aspect of this step is that the auxiliary model is not instructed to merely detect whether a concept is mentioned in an explanation. Instead, following the methodology of Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, it must determine whether the explanation attributes causal importance to the concept. Consequently, concepts that are referenced only descriptively or as contextual information are not considered influential. Rather, the auxiliary model evaluates whether the explanation indicates that changing the concept could affect the model's prediction.
+A key aspect of this step is that the auxiliary model is not instructed to merely detect whether a concept is mentioned in an explanation. Instead, following the methodology of Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, it must determine whether the explanation attributes causal importance to the concept. Consequently, concepts that are referenced only descriptively or as contextual information should not be considered influential. Rather, the auxiliary model is tasked to evaluate whether the explanation indicates that changing the concept could affect the model's prediction.
 
 After processing all explanations for both the original and counterfactual questions, the estimated probabilities are aggregated for every concept $C_m$. These probabilities represent the likelihood that the model's explanation identifies the concept as causally relevant and are subsequently used to compute the explanation-implied effects according to Equation~\ref{eq:ee}.
