 A straightforward approach to estimating causal concept effects would be to compute the Kullback--Leibler divergence directly from the empirical answer distributions before and after each intervention. However, Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} argue that this estimate becomes unreliable when only a limited number of model responses are available.
 
-To address this issue, the authors introduce a Bayesian hierarchical model that operates across the entire dataset. The underlying assumption is that concepts belonging to similar categories exhibit comparable effect magnitudes on the predictions of the LLM. Consequently, observations from related concepts and questions can be pooled, allowing more reliable estimates even when only a small number of responses has been collected for an individual question.
+To address this issue, the authors introduce a Bayesian hierarchical model that operates across the entire dataset. The underlying assumption is that concepts belonging to similar categories exhibit comparable effect magnitudes on the answer choices by the LLM. Consequently, observations from related concepts and questions can be pooled, allowing more reliable estimates even when only a small number of responses has been collected for an individual question. This improves the stability of the estimated prediction distributions.
 
-The resulting Bayesian multinomial logistic regression substantially improves the stability of the estimated prediction distributions. As the mathematical details of this model are beyond the scope of this thesis and are not directly related to the primary research topic addressed here, they are omitted but can be found in the original paper \cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}. Based on the stabilized distributions, the causal concept effects can then be computed according to Equation~\ref{eq:ce}.
+For every treatment, the model regresses the sampled answers on an indicator that separates original from counterfactual responses, using a categorical likelihood with softmax logits. The prior of each treatment coefficient has a scale that is shared by all concepts of the same category and is itself learned from the whole dataset. This partial pooling lets sparsely sampled treatments borrow strength from related ones. The Kullback--Leibler divergence in Equation~\ref{eq:ce} is computed between the two implied answer distributions for every posterior draw and then averaged. The reported CE is therefore a posterior mean rather than the divergence between relative answer frequencies. The complete specification can be found in the original paper \cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} on pages 18 to 20.
 
 \subsubsection{Estimating Causal Concept Faithfulness}
 
 The final stage of the evaluation pipeline estimates the causal concept faithfulness score. Although Equation~\ref{eq:faithfulness} defines faithfulness as the Pearson correlation coefficient between the vectors of causal concept effects and explanation-implied effects, directly computing this correlation for each question is often unreliable. Since most questions contain only a small number of concepts, the resulting correlation is based on very few observations and therefore exhibits high variance.
 
-To overcome this limitation, Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} again employ a Bayesian hierarchical model at the dataset level. The model assumes that questions within the same dataset exhibit similar degrees of explanation faithfulness, allowing statistical strength to be shared across questions. Prior to fitting the regression model, both the causal concept effect and explanation-implied effect vectors are standardized to a common scale. A global regression coefficient is then introduced as a prior for the question-specific regression coefficients. After Bayesian inference, the posterior estimates of the question-level coefficients correspond to the faithfulness scores $\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{x})$, while the posterior of the global regression coefficient represents the overall dataset-level faithfulness score $\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{X})$.
+To overcome this limitation, Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} again employ a Bayesian hierarchical model at the dataset level. The model assumes that questions within the same dataset exhibit similar degrees of explanation faithfulness, allowing statistical strength to be shared across questions. Prior to fitting the model, both the causal concept effect and explanation-implied effect vectors are standardized to a common scale. A global regression coefficient is then introduced as a prior for the question-specific regression coefficients. After Bayesian inference, the posterior estimates of the question-level coefficients correspond to the faithfulness scores $\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{x})$, while the posterior of the global regression coefficient represents the overall dataset-level faithfulness score $\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{X})$. The complete mathematical specification of this model can be found on page 20 of the paper by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}.
 
 Figure~\ref{fig:matton_evaluation_pipeline} consolidates the individual stages described above into a single overview. It emphasizes the two parallel quantities at the center of the metric: model answers are used to estimate causal effects, while natural-language explanations are used to estimate explanation-implied effects. Their alignment then determines the final faithfulness estimate.

-\subsection{The Case for this Metric}
-The Causal Concept Faithfulness metric presents several notable strengths, including its high degree of automation. Its black-box treatment of the LLM under test makes it applicable to virtually any model, regardless of internal architecture or API-only access. Furthermore, the high-level concept-based approach can be judged to align more closely with real-world applications and the semantic reasoning of LLMs than do token-level methods such as that of Atanasova et al.~\cite{atanasovaFaithfulnessTestsNatural2023}. Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} also emphasize that their metric identifies patterns of unfaithfulness, providing insight into how explanations mislead and supporting informed decisions about model deployment in given environments. This potential alone makes the metric a highly promising foundation, warranting the investment of resources to address its remaining weaknesses.
+\subsection{Limitations and the Case for this Metric}
+\label{sec:matton_limitations}
+The Causal Concept Faithfulness Metric presents several notable strengths, including its high degree of automation. Its black-box treatment of the LLM under test makes it applicable to virtually any model, regardless of internal architecture or API-only access. Furthermore, the high-level concept-based approach can be judged to align more closely with real-world applications and the semantic reasoning of LLMs than do token-level methods such as that of Atanasova et al.~\cite{atanasovaFaithfulnessTestsNatural2023}. Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} also emphasize that their metric identifies patterns of unfaithfulness, providing insight into how explanations mislead and supporting informed decisions about model deployment in given environments. This potential alone makes the metric a highly promising foundation, warranting the investment of resources to address its remaining weaknesses.
 
-For instance, many limitations stem from its reliance on LLM-based automation. Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} acknowledge that even a capable proprietary auxiliary model, \texttt{GPT-4o}, at times introduced errors into the pipeline. In concept extraction, all identified concepts were present in the question, but not all satisfied the disentanglement constraint. Counterfactual generation also exhibited some deficits, including the unintended modification of non-target concepts and the inconsistent replacement of a concept mentioned in multiple locations. Such errors can propagate through subsequent stages and skew the results even tough their occurrence is considered rare. More generally, the need to adapt auxiliary LLM prompts to specific datasets was identified as a practical disadvantage by the authors themselves.
+For instance, many limitations stem from its reliance on LLM-based automation. Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} acknowledge that even a capable proprietary auxiliary model, GPT-4o, at times introduced errors into the pipeline. In concept extraction, all identified concepts were present in the question, but not all satisfied the disentanglement constraint. Counterfactual generation also exhibited some deficits, including the unintended modification of non-target concepts and the inconsistent replacement of a concept mentioned in multiple locations. Such errors can propagate through subsequent stages and skew the results even though their occurrence is considered rare. More generally, the need to adapt auxiliary LLM prompts to specific datasets was identified as a practical disadvantage by the authors themselves.
 
-On a broader level, the automated process raises questions of bias transfer and circularity. If the auxiliary LLM $\mathcal{A}$ possesses its own biases, expressed through concept identification and counterfactual generation, these may be superimposed upon the biases of the model $\mathcal{M}$ under evaluation. Given the central role of $\mathcal{A}$, the pipeline effectively uses one LLM to evaluate another, which invites scrutiny as to whether true faithfulness is being measured or merely the alignment between the two models' biases. Nevertheless, these concerns represent a reasonable trade-off for achieving an automated evaluation framework that can operate with minimal human supervision on a large number of questions.
+More broadly, the automation that reduces the need for human supervision raises concerns about bias transfer and circularity. If the auxiliary LLM $\mathcal{A}$ possesses its own biases, expressed through concept identification and counterfactual generation, these may be superimposed upon the biases of the model $\mathcal{M}$ under evaluation. Given the central role of $\mathcal{A}$, the pipeline effectively uses one LLM to evaluate another, which invites scrutiny as to whether true faithfulness is being measured or merely the alignment between the two models' biases. Choosing an auxiliary model that differs from $\mathcal{M}$ mitigates this risk, although models from related families or trained on comparable data may still share biases.
 
-However, another limitation still threatens the practical utility of the Causal Concept Faithfulness metric: the Correlated Concepts Problem. Because defining and identifying this issue forms the foundation of this thesis, it warrants a dedicated chapter.
+Another limitation still threatens the practical utility of the Causal Concept Faithfulness Metric: the Correlated Concepts Problem. Because defining and identifying this issue forms the foundation of this thesis, it warrants a dedicated chapter.

 \section{Summary of the Thesis}
 
-This thesis investigated how causal concept faithfulness can be measured when concepts in an input cannot be treated as independent units of evidence. The starting point was the Causal Concept Faithfulness metric proposed by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, which compares the influence an LLM attributes to concepts in its explanation with causal effects estimated through individual interventions. Atomic interventions can, however, underestimate or misallocate influence when one concept can be reconstructed from another or when concepts affect an answer jointly.
+This thesis investigated how causal concept faithfulness can be measured when concepts in an input cannot be treated as independent units of evidence. The starting point was the Causal Concept Faithfulness Metric proposed by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, which compares the influence an LLM attributes to concepts in its explanation with causal effects estimated through individual interventions. Atomic interventions can, however, underestimate or misallocate influence when one concept can be reconstructed from another or when concepts affect an answer jointly.
 
 To address this limitation, the thesis introduced FELICS (Faithfulness Estimation Leveraging Interacting Concept Sets). An oracle partitions concepts into bounded candidate interaction groups, after which every non-empty coalition within each group is evaluated. Shapley values allocate the observed coalition effects to individual concepts, preserving the concept-level comparison between causal and explanation-implied effects. A reference-relative correction addresses systematic departures from additivity on the selected effect scale, while $n_{\max}$ limits the exponential growth in counterfactuals. FELICS was formalized, implemented, and evaluated on BBQ, MedQA, and the newly constructed tabular dataset New German Credit. The oracle acts as a screening mechanism: it proposes relationships worth examining, while counterfactual model responses provide the measured coalition effects.
 
 \section{Answers to the Research Questions}
 
-Five research questions where defined in Section \ref{sec:research_questions} before collecting data with the reference implementation. The evaluation of \rqref{rq:correlation_diff} did not establish a general association between oracle-derived interaction-presumption strength and changes in causal effects or faithfulness. Larger or more numerous candidate groups therefore only indicate greater scope for joint evaluation, rather than demonstrating greater response-level interaction.
+Four research questions were defined in Chapter \ref{sec:research_questions} before collecting data with the reference implementation; in addition, the MedQA example that motivated this thesis was revisited as a case study. The evaluation of \rqref{rq:correlation_diff} did not establish a general association between oracle-derived interaction-presumption strength and changes in causal effects or faithfulness. Larger or more numerous candidate groups therefore only indicate greater scope for joint evaluation, rather than demonstrating greater response-level interaction.
 
-For \rqref{rq:convergence}, FELICS is theoretically compatible with the original metric when $n_{\max}=1$: under matching inputs and effect estimates, its construction reduces to that of Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} as shown in Math Demo \ref{ma:independence_behavior}. The independently executed pipelines did not establish empirical equivalence within the predefined bounds. Although both used removal-based interventions, separate runs still varied through concept decomposition, counterfactual wording, sampled answers, and explanation interpretation. Practical reproducibility consequently remains unresolved and should be tested with fixed intermediate inputs and model outputs.
+For \rqref{rq:convergence}, FELICS is theoretically compatible with the original metric when $n_{\max}=1$: under matching inputs and effect estimates, its construction reduces to that of Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} as shown in Math Demo \ref{ma:one_item_group}. The independently executed pipelines did not establish empirical equivalence within the predefined bounds. Although both used the same intervention mode for each dataset, separate runs still varied through concept decomposition, counterfactual wording, sampled answers, and explanation interpretation. Practical reproducibility consequently remains unresolved and should be tested with fixed intermediate inputs and model outputs.
 
 The results for \rqref{rq:ranking} show that the reference correction is more than a uniform rescaling. It broadly preserved concept rankings in BBQ and MedQA, but produced more extensive reordering in New German Credit.
 
 For \rqref{rq:faithfulness_increase}, joint interventions did not systematically increase question-level faithfulness. The direction of change varied between dataset--model combinations. FELICS can revise which concepts receive causal credit, but faithfulness increases only when the revised CE vector aligns more closely with the EE vector. Interaction-aware attribution can accordingly reveal greater, lower, or similar explanation--behavior alignment.
 
-Finally, the conjunctive hypothesis for \rqref{rq:motivating_example} was not supported. Joint attribution increased the effects assigned to only one of the two investigated concepts for both evaluated models. The result nevertheless demonstrated that coalition measurements can materially redistribute causal credit.
+Finally, the expectation for the MedQA case study was only partially met. Joint attribution increased the effects assigned to only one of the two investigated concepts for both evaluated models. The result nevertheless demonstrated that coalition measurements can materially redistribute causal credit.
 
 \section{Research Contribution}
 
-The principal conceptual contribution of this thesis is to make the correlated-concepts problem an explicit component of  faithfulness measurement within the Counterfactual Edits line of work. FELICS distinguishes relationships presumed by an oracle from interactions expressed in the evaluated model's answer distribution. Statistical association and semantic similarity can thereby guide the allocation of a limited counterfactual budget without being interpreted as evidence of interaction themselves.
+The principal conceptual contribution of this thesis is to make the Correlated Concepts Problem an explicit component of faithfulness measurement within the Counterfactual Edits line of work. FELICS distinguishes relationships presumed by an oracle from interactions expressed in the evaluated model's answer distribution. Statistical association and semantic similarity can thereby guide the allocation of a limited counterfactual budget without being interpreted as evidence of interaction themselves.
 
-Methodologically, FELICS adds bounded joint interventions and Shapley-based attribution to Causal Concept Faithfulness while retaining its individual concept representation. The empirical examples show the value of this extension. Joint removal can reveal threshold-like behaviour obscured by singleton interventions. It can also partially mitigate an overly fine concept decomposition: if the auxiliary model extracts overlapping descriptions of the same evidence and the oracle groups them, their coalition effect can limit the extent to which they are treated as independent sources of influence. Other cases show that a plausible group may exhibit little consequential interaction, or that a large coalition effect may be confounded by unintended counterfactual edits. FELICS makes these behaviours inspectable, but does not determine their interpretation automatically.
+Methodologically, FELICS adds bounded joint interventions and Shapley-based attribution to Causal Concept Faithfulness while retaining its individual concept representation. The empirical examples show the value of this extension. Joint removal can reveal threshold-like behavior obscured by singleton interventions. It can also partially mitigate an overly fine concept decomposition: if the auxiliary model extracts overlapping descriptions of the same evidence and the oracle groups them, their coalition effect can limit the extent to which they are treated as independent sources of influence. Other cases show that a plausible group may exhibit little consequential interaction, or that a large coalition effect may be confounded by unintended counterfactual edits. FELICS makes these behaviors inspectable, but does not determine their interpretation automatically.
 
 The thesis also contributes a reference implementation for natural-language and tabular inputs and corresponding oracle variants. Its evaluation demonstrates local attribution changes that atomic interventions alone cannot reveal, while rejecting the stronger expectation that interaction awareness must produce higher aggregate faithfulness. FELICS preserves concept-level outputs and was computationally feasible at the evaluated values of $n_{\max}$. Its scope remains bounded: singleton interventions are still required for Shapley attribution, interactions across group boundaries remain unmeasured, and normalization makes the final attribution reference-relative. FELICS should therefore be understood as a practical interaction-aware diagnostic framework rather than a procedure for recovering a unique ground-truth interaction structure.
 
@@ -37,4 +37,4 @@ Further work should validate the oracle and attribution procedure using question
 
 The reference correction also requires sensitivity analysis. Reporting raw and adjusted Shapley allocations together would expose its influence, while partially pooled reference estimates may improve stability when few comparable treatments exist. A graded EE estimator could additionally preserve distinctions between moderate and decisive influence, provided that the scale and auxiliary judgments are validated.
 
-Measuring explanation faithfulness requires attention to how concepts - or more broadly parts of input - overlap, substitute for one another, and matter in combination. FELICS provides a concrete framework for incorporating these relationships into faithfulness measurement by buil6ding on the work by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}. The evaluation shows that joint interventions expose causal attribution patterns that atomic interventions can conceal, while also revealing the dependence of those patterns on concept representation, counterfactual construction, grouping, and normalization. These findings provide a basis for faithfulness metrics that represent the structure of inputs to a LLM more adequately and therefore provide richer estimates.
+Measuring explanation faithfulness requires attention to how concepts (or, more broadly, parts of the input) overlap, substitute for one another, and matter in combination. FELICS provides a concrete framework for incorporating these relationships into faithfulness measurement by building on the work by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}. The evaluation shows that joint interventions expose causal attribution patterns that atomic interventions can conceal, while also revealing the dependence of those patterns on concept representation, counterfactual construction, grouping, and normalization. These findings provide a basis for faithfulness metrics that represent the structure of inputs to an LLM more adequately and therefore provide richer estimates.

 FELICS extends causal concept faithfulness measurement by evaluating selected concepts jointly and allocating the resulting effects back to individual concepts. The results in Chapter~\ref{sec:results} show that this extension can materially change the attribution of causal influence within a question. They do not establish that accounting for these effects systematically increases measured faithfulness. This distinction is central to interpreting the evaluation: a revised estimate of causal importance can agree either more or less closely with the influence claimed in an explanation.

 The controlled atomic--joint comparisons and the comparison with the original implementation answer different questions. The former reuse collected interventions and responses to isolate the consequences of joint attribution, keeping the EE vector fixed. The latter also reflect variation between independently generated pipeline inputs and model outputs. Similarity between atomic and joint FELICS scores therefore does not independently validate compatibility with the original implementation.

-Comparisons between a coalition effect and the sum of its singleton effects describe super- or sub-additivity on the chosen effect scale. +Comparisons between a coalition effect and the sum of its singleton effects describe super- or subadditivity on the chosen effect scale. They do not by themselves prove substantive synergy or redundancy. The nonlinear effect estimator, sampling variation, and unintended counterfactual edits can also contribute to the observed pattern. The accompanying question context and answer distributions help assess plausible interpretations.
 
 \subsection{A Threshold-Like Response in New German Credit}
 
-\begin{example_box}[title={Interaction case \thetcbcounter: New German Credit, example 4},label={ex:new_german_credit_threshold},fontupper=\small,arc=2mm,boxrule=0.3mm,breakable=false]
-	\textbf{Model:} GPT-OSS 120B.
-
-	\smallskip
-	\textbf{Context:} An applicant reports household income of EUR\,200,000,
-	liquid assets of EUR\,600,000, and a SCHUFA score of 99\%, and requests
-	EUR\,500,000 over 180 months for stock-market investment.
-
-	\smallskip
-	\textbf{Coalition $T$:} income, liquid assets, and SCHUFA score.
-	\[
-		\begin{array}{lr}
-			\text{Singleton: income}               & 0.185 \\
-			\text{Singleton: liquid assets}        & 0.090 \\
-			\text{Singleton: SCHUFA score}         & 0.108 \\
-			\hline
-			\sum_{C_i\in T}v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_i\}) & 0.384 \\
-			v_{\mathbf{x}}(T)                      & 1.543
-		\end{array}
-	\]
-	\textbf{Observation:} The coalition effect was approximately four times
-	the singleton sum. The reported sum uses the underlying estimates;
-	the displayed components are rounded.
-\end{example_box}
-
-The tabular oracle grouped these three financial indicators together (Figure~\ref{fig:german-credit-grouping-final}). Making one unavailable left two favorable indicators on which the model could still base its assessment. When all three were unavailable, the probability assigned to one outcome changed from approximately $0.77$ under control to $0.02$ under treatment. This pattern is suggestive of a threshold mechanism: the remaining indicators can sustain the original assessment until their joint removal changes the balance of evidence. Singleton effects alone consequently give an incomplete picture of the response to removing the full coalition. This interpretation concerns the evaluated model's response to the constructed applicant, rather than a causal relationship in an underlying population of borrowers.
+In \caseref{ex:new_german_credit_threshold}, making one of the three grouped financial indicators unavailable left two favorable indicators on which the model could still base its assessment. Only the joint removal of all three changed the predicted outcome substantially. This pattern is suggestive of a threshold mechanism: the remaining indicators can sustain the original assessment until their joint removal changes the balance of evidence.  \subsection{Concept Decomposition with an Ambiguous Removal Effect}
 
-\begin{example_box}[title={Interaction case \thetcbcounter: BBQ, question 18},label={ex:bbq_concept_decomposition},fontupper=\small,arc=2mm,boxrule=0.3mm,breakable=false]

-The extracted concepts describe highly overlapping sentence-level evidence: the individuals’ actions express their stated clothing preferences. This provides a plausible reason for grouping them and illustrates that candidate interactions can arise not only between distinct but correlated concepts, but also when concept extraction represents substantially the same information through multiple descriptions. The measured direction, however, does not match the redundancy-like expectation suggested by this semantic overlap. The raw effect of removing both concepts jointly was $0.893$, substantially below the sum of their singleton effects ($1.599$). As established in Section~\ref{sec:interaction_effects}, such subadditivity corresponds to synergy-like behavior under the removal-intervention interpretation. The reference-relative correction increased the joint value only modestly, using a payout reference coefficient of $0.984$; it therefore cannot account for the gap to the singleton sum. Its Shapley allocation yielded adjusted concept effects of $0.443$ for actions and $0.453$ for preferences.

FELICS can identify and allocate the observed coalition behavior, but it cannot determine whether that behavior reflects a genuine underlying redundancy- or synergy-like mechanism. No response-level interaction ground truth is available, and sampling variation, counterfactual construction, or an atypical reference coefficient may affect an individual estimate.

 \section{Interpreting the Mixed Research Findings}
 \label{sec:mixed_research_findings}
 
-The research questions initially defined in Section \ref{sec:research_questions} address complementary aspects of the proposed extension, from the relationship between oracle groupings and measured changes to compatibility, normalization, and the motivating example. The following subsections interpret these findings together, separating conclusions supported by the evaluation from explanations that remain provisional.
+The research questions initially defined in Chapter \ref{sec:research_questions} address complementary aspects of the proposed extension, from the relationship between oracle groupings and measured changes to compatibility, normalization, and faithfulness. The following subsections interpret these findings together with those of the MedQA case study, separating conclusions supported by the evaluation from explanations that remain provisional.
 
 \subsection{From Oracle Presumptions to Changes in Faithfulness}
 
-Example~\ref{ex:bbq_low_interaction} helps explain the findings for \rqref{rq:correlation_diff}. As operationalized in Section \ref{sec:analysis_procedures}, the measure $\Delta\kappa$ reflects the number and size of groups proposed by the oracle, and hence the additional coalitions to be evaluated. It does not incorporate the evaluated model's response. A larger value therefore indicates a more extensive presumption of interaction, without establishing that the resulting effects will be large. The positive MedQA association for GPT-OSS ($r=0.338$, $p=0.036$) in Section \ref{sec:results-interaction-presumption} provides limited support for the expected relationship with changes in CE. The weak BBQ relationships and absence of consistent faithfulness associations suggest that sampling uncertainty alone is unlikely to explain the mixed findings.
+\caseref{ex:bbq_low_interaction} helps explain the findings for \rqref{rq:correlation_diff}. As operationalized in Section \ref{sec:analysis_procedures}, the measure $\Delta\kappa$ reflects the number and size of groups proposed by the oracle, and hence the additional coalitions to be evaluated. It does not incorporate the evaluated model's response. A larger value therefore indicates a more extensive presumption of interaction, without establishing that the resulting effects will be large. The positive MedQA association for GPT-OSS 120B ($r=0.338$, $p=0.036$) in Section \ref{sec:results-interaction-presumption} provides limited support for the expected relationship with changes in CE. This association weakens to $r=0.167$ without question 434, while question 1120 combines the largest $\Delta\kappa$ with almost no change in CE. $\Delta\kappa$ therefore indicates where joint attribution can matter rather than how much it changes a result. With 24 to 30 questions per combination, sampling uncertainty remains a plausible contributor to the mixed findings, and the absence of a supported association does not show that none exists.
 
-The results for \rqref{rq:faithfulness_increase} (Section \ref{sec:results-faithfulness-increase}) reinforce the distinction between causal attribution and explanation alignment. Joint effects can alter individual CE values substantially while leaving much of the CE--EE relationship intact. New German Credit example 4 illustrates this: despite substantial changes in the allocations to income, assets, and SCHUFA score, question-level faithfulness decreased from $0.363$ under atomic attribution to $0.272$ under joint attribution. The effect on correlation depends on how all concept values move relative to EE; neither a larger coalition effect nor increased CE for several concepts determines its direction.
+The results for \rqref{rq:faithfulness_increase} (Section \ref{sec:results-faithfulness-increase}) reinforce the distinction between causal attribution and explanation alignment. Joint effects can alter individual CE values substantially while leaving much of the CE--EE relationship intact. \caseref{ex:new_german_credit_threshold} illustrates this: despite substantial changes in the allocations to income, assets, and SCHUFA score, question-level faithfulness decreased from $0.363$ under atomic attribution to $0.272$ under joint attribution. The effect on correlation depends on how all concept values move relative to EE. Neither a larger coalition effect nor increased CE for several concepts determines its direction.
 
-The pre-specified increase criterion was met for Mistral on New German Credit, while complementary tests indicated decreases for both models on BBQ. These opposing directions do not support a universal increase under the evaluated conditions. The decrease tests were exploratory and unadjusted for multiple comparisons, so their nominal significance is better treated as a basis for further investigation than as confirmatory evidence. Normalization may contribute to some of the observed shifts, but the experiment did not isolate its contribution to the direction of faithfulness change.
+The prespecified increase criterion was met for Mistral Medium 3.5 on New German Credit, while complementary tests indicated decreases for both models on BBQ. These opposing directions do not support a universal increase under the evaluated conditions. The increase for Mistral Medium 3.5 on New German Credit also starts from a negative value. Under atomic attribution, this model spreads CE almost evenly across concepts, including concepts its explanations rarely mention. Joint attribution leaves the question-averaged CE nearly unchanged but redistributes it along the oracle groups (Section~\ref{sec:normalization_and_rankings}), which are not derived from the explanations. This weakens the negative relationship without producing a positive one ($-0.271$ to $-0.140$, with an interval that still contains zero), so the increase is better described as reduced misalignment than as improved faithfulness. The decrease tests were exploratory and unadjusted for multiple comparisons, so their nominal significance is better treated as a basis for further investigation than as confirmatory evidence. Normalization may contribute to some of the observed shifts, but the experiment did not isolate its contribution to the direction of faithfulness change.
 
 \subsection{Singleton Compatibility and Independent Pipeline Runs}
 \label{sec:singleton_compatibility}
 
-For \rqref{rq:convergence}, both compared pipelines used removal-based interventions. The singleton compatibility established in Math Demo~\ref{ma:one_item_group} concerns the metric under matching inputs and effect estimates. Independently executing the pipelines additionally introduces variation in concept extraction, counterfactual wording, sampled answers, and explanation interpretation. Even with the same intervention mode, natural-language removal can operate on different concept decompositions and produce different remaining text. This manifests itself in the results in Section \ref{sec:results-original-validation}.
+For \rqref{rq:convergence}, both compared pipelines used the same intervention mode for each dataset. The singleton compatibility established in Math Demo~\ref{ma:one_item_group} concerns the metric under matching inputs and effect estimates. Independently executing the pipelines additionally introduces variation in concept extraction, counterfactual wording, sampled answers, and explanation interpretation. Even with the same intervention mode, natural-language interventions can operate on different concept decompositions and produce different counterfactual text. This manifests itself in the results in Section \ref{sec:results-original-validation}.
 
-The qualitative inspection of BBQ question 29 (Example \ref{ex:bbq_low_interaction}) illustrates this upstream sensitivity. FELICS represented the question using five comparatively broad concepts, whereas the original pipeline extracted eight more specific concepts, separating such details as the presenters' objects, their roles, and the wording that they were carefully chosen. These decompositions assign effects to different units of evidence. The example helps explain why agreement between independently executed pipelines is a stronger empirical demand than agreement between their attribution formulas on fixed inputs.
+The qualitative inspection of BBQ question 29 (\caseref{ex:bbq_low_interaction}) illustrates this upstream sensitivity. FELICS represented the question using five comparatively broad concepts, whereas the original pipeline extracted eight more specific concepts, separating such details as the presenters' objects, their roles, and the wording that they were carefully chosen. These decompositions assign effects to different units of evidence. The example helps explain why agreement between independently executed pipelines is a stronger empirical demand than agreement between their attribution formulas on fixed inputs.
 
 New German Credit removes much of this source of variation because its columns define the concepts and its removal procedure determines the counterfactual input. Nevertheless, only five responses were sampled per question variant. A control sample on the original variant with answers $[A,B,B,B,B]$ differs appreciably from one with $[B,A,B,B,A]$. Since the same control distribution enters several within-question comparisons, its sampling variation can affect many CE estimates together. Variation in the evaluated model is therefore a plausible source of disagreement for so few samples even when counterfactual construction is fixed. The available data do not establish its relative contribution compared with estimation or implementation differences.
 
-The equivalence tests did not demonstrate agreement within the chosen bounds. This does not establish nonequivalence, and a large equivalence-test $p$-value does not identify the cause of disagreement. Complementary difference tests address a different null hypothesis; they do not test statistical independence. For New German Credit, both models show significant CE and faithfulness differences under the unadjusted complementary tests. FELICS has lower mean CE for GPT-OSS 120B and higher mean CE for Mistral Medium 3.5, while mean faithfulness is higher for both models. These exploratory results should be interpreted alongside the equivalence tests rather than as a substitute for them. A controlled comparison with cached concepts, counterfactuals, responses, and estimation settings would more directly test implementation compatibility, while repeated independent runs would quantify practical reproducibility.
+Two independent FELICS runs indicate how much agreement can be expected at all. Although both rely only on singleton interventions, their question-level faithfulness estimates correlate at only $0.06$ to $0.54$ (Section~\ref{sec:results-original-validation}). This exceeds the agreement with the original implementation but remains low for MedQA and New German Credit. With a median credible-interval width of about $2.4$, question-level faithfulness is too variable for independent runs to agree on individual questions. Part of the disagreement between the implementations therefore reflects the variability of the measurement rather than a difference specific to FELICS. This variability also widens the spread of the paired differences, which makes equivalence within the chosen margins hard to establish.
+
+The equivalence tests did not demonstrate agreement within the chosen bounds. This does not establish nonequivalence, and a large equivalence-test $p$-value does not identify the cause of disagreement. The exploratory difference tests address a different null hypothesis and find no combination that differs significantly in both outcomes, so they complement the equivalence tests rather than replace them. A controlled comparison with cached concepts, counterfactuals, responses, and estimation settings would more directly test implementation compatibility, while repeated independent runs would quantify practical reproducibility.
 
 \subsection{Normalization and Preservation of Concept Rankings}
 \label{sec:normalization_and_rankings}
 
 For \rqref{rq:ranking}, the pooled concept-level rank correlations exceeded the predefined threshold for both natural-language datasets and models as documented in Section~\ref{sec:results-ranking-preservation}. This supports broad preservation of ordering in those conditions, but not exact preservation within every question. New German Credit fell below the threshold for both models and had no question with completely unchanged ranks. The contrast is therefore one of degree and consistency, rather than a sharp separation between a distortion-free natural-language setting and a distorted tabular setting.
 
-Reference construction offers one possible explanation. The correction in Section~\ref{sec:reference_set} prioritizes other treatments of the same size. For a three-concept grand coalition in New German Credit, only two other grand coalitions are available within the question. This is illustrated by the partitioning graph in Figure~\ref{fig:german-credit-grouping-final}. Additional references must be drawn from other questions to reach the desired reference-set size. Although this increases the number of observations, it repeatedly draws on a limited range of coalition types. Reference size and substantive diversity are therefore distinct. Because Shapley efficiency ties total allocation to the grand-coalition value, its correction can influence the allocation to every participating concept.
+Reference construction offers one possible explanation. The correction in Section~\ref{sec:reference_set} prioritizes other treatments of the same size. For a three-concept grand coalition in New German Credit, only two other grand coalitions are available within the question. This is illustrated by the partitioning graph in Figure~\ref{fig:german-credit-grouping-final}. Additional references must be drawn from other questions to reach the desired reference-set size. Although this increases the number of observations, it repeatedly draws on a limited range of coalition types. Reference size and substantive diversity are therefore distinct. Because Shapley efficiency ties total allocation to the grand-coalition value, its correction can influence the allocation to every participating concept. The concept-level adjustments fit this explanation. For Mistral Medium 3.5, they are nearly constant within each group (Figure~\ref{fig:rq3-delta-credit-mistral}), so the correction acts like a lift for the whole group, and a concept's rank after normalization depends more on its group than on its own effect. Because this model's atomic causal effects differ little between concepts, such a lift easily reorders them. For GPT-OSS 120B, only one group receives a large adjustment, which leaves more of the original ranking intact.
 
 This explanation remains provisional. A useful alternative for tabular data would construct references from the same feature coalition across other rows, for example comparing income, assets, and SCHUFA with that same combination elsewhere. Such matching may improve substantive comparability, but could also make attributions more explicitly relative to typical behavior of that coalition. It would require evaluation rather than being assumed to preserve ranks or improve validity.
 
-\subsection{The Motivating Underestimation Example}
+\subsection{The MedQA Case Study}

 \section{Requirements Revisited}
 \label{sec:requirements_revisited}
-The empirical evaluation refines the theoretical assessment introduced in Section~\ref{sec:post_model_assessment}. Although all six FELICS requirements were previously evaluated using the model, experimental data provides fresh insights.
+The empirical evaluation refines the theoretical assessment introduced in Section~\ref{sec:requirements_reexamination}. Although all five FELICS requirements were previously evaluated using the model, experimental data provides fresh insights.
 
 \paragraph{Preservation of concept-level interpretability.}
 \reqref{req:preserving_strengths} is retained structurally: FELICS still produces separate causal and explanation-implied effects for individual concepts. The examples show the analytical value of this representation. Its interpretability nevertheless depends on the quality of the concept decomposition and on understanding adjusted effects as reference-relative quantities.
 
-\paragraph{Singleton and additivity compatibility.}
-\reqref{req:one_item_group} is established theoretically under matching inputs (Math Demo~\ref{ma:one_item_group} in Appendix~\ref{app:mathematical_demonstrations}); reproducibility across independent runs remains empirically unresolved. For \reqref{req:independence_behavior}, the mathematical result applies under the stated reference-relative additivity assumptions. The near-unchanged effects in Example~\ref{ex:bbq_low_interaction} are consistent with the intended behaviour, but cannot validate it generally without an interaction ground truth. At the aggregate level, the largely similar distributions of question-level causal effects shown in Figure~\ref{app:ce-shift-rainclouds} are likewise consistent with the anticipated preservation of dataset-level mean CE derived in Math Demo~\ref{ma:dataset_average_preservation}. This is supportive but not confirmatory evidence: aggregate similarity can conceal compensating concept-level reallocations and does not establish the absence of omitted interactions.
+\paragraph{Singleton compatibility.}
+\reqref{req:one_item_group} is established theoretically under matching inputs (Math Demo~\ref{ma:one_item_group}), but reproducibility across independent runs is limited at the question level, even between two FELICS runs (Section~\ref{sec:singleton_compatibility}).
 
 \paragraph{Intervention validity and interaction coverage.}
-\reqref{req:no_surgical} is partially satisfied. Joint interventions reduce exclusive reliance on atomic interventions, but singleton values remain necessary for Shapley allocation, and unintended edits remain possible. \reqref{req:caputure_interaction} is likewise only partially satisfied. FELICS identifies candidate groups and measures their coalition effects under a specified intervention procedure and effect scale; it can therefore expose super- or subadditive response patterns that singleton interventions cannot observe. It is not, however, an absolute detector of redundancy or synergy. Semantic overlap, oracle groupings, and observed coalition effects need not imply the same interaction direction, as illustrated by Example~\ref{ex:bbq_concept_decomposition}. Moreover, no external interaction ground truth is available, while sampling variation, counterfactual validity, group boundaries, and reference-relative normalization can affect an individual estimate. FELICS consequently provides an interaction-aware attribution framework and diagnostic that allocates observed coalition effect fairly, rather than a method for recovering an inherent or uniquely correct interaction type.
+\reqref{req:no_surgical} is partially satisfied. It can therefore expose super- or subadditive response patterns that singleton interventions cannot observe. FELICS consequently provides an interaction-aware attribution framework and diagnostic that allocates observed coalition effect fairly, rather than a method for recovering an inherent or uniquely correct interaction type.
 
 \paragraph{Computational feasibility.}
 The completed experiments provide practical support for \reqref{req:feasibility} at the evaluated scale and chosen values of $n_{\max}$. The group-size bound made evaluation possible within the available resources. Without a systematic runtime or cost benchmark, this should not be generalized to larger concept sets, richer replacement schemes, or substantially larger sample sizes.

 \subsection{Candidate Groups and Unmeasured Interactions}
 
-Statistical association concerns the joint distribution of inputs. Response-level interaction concerns whether the effect of changing one concept depends on the intervention status of another. Association can make informational overlap plausible, but does not establish interaction in the evaluated model; interaction can also occur without strong pairwise association. The tabular oracle uses pairwise Cram\'er's $V$ and does not condition on the model's response. +Statistical association concerns the joint distribution of inputs. Response-level interaction concerns whether the effect of changing one concept depends on the intervention status of another. The natural-language oracle is prompted to identify redundancy-like and synergy-like relationships, but can still rely on semantic associations and learned co-occurrence patterns. Both therefore propose candidates rather than identify a verified interaction structure.
 
 The screening stage can group concepts with little additional joint effect or separate concepts with consequential interactions. The hard group-size bound can create further separations. Evaluating every coalition within each selected group does not recover interactions across group boundaries. Thus the coverage of FELICS depends on both the oracle and the chosen computational budget, and increasing $n_{\max}$ may not by itself guarantee an improved partition or a more valid attribution.

 \chapter{Evaluation Methodology}
 \label{ch:evaluation_methodology}
-The preceding chapters have modeled the proposed extension FELICS from a purely theoretical standpoint: not only were the principles guiding its design established in Chapter~\ref{sec:conception}, but its behavior was also formalized and predicted in Chapter~\ref{sec:modeling_updated_metric}. However, it remains to be shown whether the metric behaves in practice as its theoretical construction suggests. To this end, a thorough evaluation is conducted against a reference implementation, in which several central research questions are addressed and the resulting data and findings are processed and visualized.
-
-\section{Research Questions}
-\label{sec:research_questions}
-
-To empirically evaluate the efficacy, theoretical soundness, and practical implications of the proposed FELICS framework, the experimental design pursued in this chapter is guided by five research questions, each formulated as a testable hypothesis. Taken together, these hypotheses help to assess whether the implementation successfully fulfills the formalized requirements (see Section~\ref{sec:requirements}) and gauge which behaviors the metric extension exhibits in practice.
-
-\begin{enumerate}[
-		label=\textbf{Q\arabic*},
-		ref=Q\arabic*,
-		wide=0pt,
-		leftmargin=0pt
-	]
-	\item \label{rq:correlation_diff} \textbf{Impact of Interaction-Presumption Strength on Causal Effect Estimates}
-
-	      \noindent\textit{Research question.} Are stronger oracle presumptions of concept interaction associated with larger differences in Causal Effect (CE) estimates when interactions are modeled explicitly?
-
-	      \noindent\textit{Hypothesis.} As established in Section~\ref{sec:joint_counterfactual}, the oracle $\mathcal{O}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{C},n_{\max})$ partitions the concept set $\mathbf{C}$ of a question $\mathbf{x}$ into candidate interaction groups. Larger or more numerous groups indicate a stronger oracle presumption that concepts should be evaluated jointly; they do not constitute a measurement of response-level interaction. It is hypothesized that the difference between a concept's atomic effect and its interaction-adjusted effect correlates positively with the corresponding difference in interaction-presumption strength.
-
-	\item \label{rq:convergence} \textbf{Convergence to the Baseline Metric}
-
-	      \noindent\textit{Research question.} Does FELICS, when restricted to atomic interventions, converge to the original Causal Concept Faithfulness metric not only in theory but also in practically observed results?
-
-	      \noindent\textit{Hypothesis.} Math Demo~\ref{ma:one_item_group} in Appendix~\ref{app:mathematical_demonstrations} points out that whenever every candidate interaction group contains exactly one concept, the causal-effect formula of FELICS reduces exactly to that of the original metric of Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, thereby meeting the singleton-group compatibility criterion specified by \reqref{req:one_item_group}, introduced in Section~\ref{sec:requirements}. Because FELICS is nevertheless evaluated using a separate implementation, this theoretical equivalence must still be verified: discrepancies could arise from accidental changes that break compatibility. It is therefore hypothesized that FELICS with $n_{\max}=1$ agrees numerically with the original implementation, and that independently sampled runs remain equivalent within prespecified practically negligible margins at both the CE and question-level faithfulness levels.
-
-	\item \label{rq:ranking} \textbf{Preservation of Within-Question Concept Rankings}
-
-	      \noindent\textit{Research question.} Does the payout-coefficient correction applied by FELICS preserve the relative ranking of causal effect attributes to concepts within a question?
-
-	      \noindent\textit{Hypothesis.} \reqref{req:caputure_interaction} from Section~\ref{sec:requirements} demands that interaction effects be quantified and the resulting causal influence attributed to the participating concepts in a principled manner. As outlined in Section \ref{sec:shapley_correction}, FELICS applies a payout-coefficient correction to coalition values to address systematic scale differences between singleton and joint interventions, and then computes concept-level attributions through Shapley values using the corrected value function. This raises the question of whether the resulting attributions preserve the relative importance of concepts implied by Shapley values computed from the uncorrected value function or substantially change their ordering within a question. It is hypothesized that, within a question, the rank order of concepts obtained from the adjusted causal effect $\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}$ correlates strongly and positively with the rank order obtained from the raw, uncorrected Shapley causal effect $\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{raw}}$---that is, the payout-coefficient correction rescales the magnitude of each concept's estimated effect without substantially reordering which concepts appear most effectful.
-
-	\item \label{rq:faithfulness_increase} \textbf{Enhancement of Faithfulness Scores through Interaction Modeling}
-
-	      \noindent\textit{Research question.} Does capturing interaction effects between concepts yield higher measured faithfulness for the evaluated models?
-
-	      \noindent\textit{Hypothesis.} Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} report that all LLMs evaluated received positive dataset-level faithfulness scores on the chosen benchmark datasets, suggesting the models already behave in a relatively faithful manner. However, because their original metric assumes disentangled, non-interacting concepts, it cannot capture cases in which a genuinely faithful model's explanation reflects the joint, rather than individual, influence of correlated concepts (cf.\ Section~\ref{sec:remaining_pipeline}). This mismatch may artificially widen the gap between the Causal Effect and the Explanation-Implied Effect, thereby penalizing an otherwise faithful model with an unfairly low faithfulness score. Since FELICS is designed to close exactly this gap, and given that evaluated LLMs may generally be assumed to be relatively faithful on average, it is hypothesized that question-level faithfulness scores are significantly higher once interaction effects are considered than under the atomic baseline.
-
-	\item \label{rq:motivating_example} \textbf{Resolution of the Motivating Underestimation Example}
-
-	      \noindent\textit{Research question.} Does FELICS resolve the specific underestimation identified as motivation for this thesis?
-
-	      \noindent\textit{Hypothesis.} As discussed in Section~\ref{sec:pratical_limit}, Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} identify a MedQA question in which the concepts \textit{the patient's eating disorder} and \textit{the patient's self-perception of weight} each receive a surprisingly low Causal Concept Effect, likely because the evaluated LLM can reconstruct either concept from the other whenever only one of them is removed. Section~\ref{sec:interaction_effects} establishes that, under the removal-based intervention scheme adopted for this dataset, this behavior constitutes redundancy, characterized by $J_{\mathbf{x}}(\{A,B\}) > J_{\mathbf{x}}(\{A\}) + J_{\mathbf{x}}(\{B\})$ for the two concepts $A$ and $B$. Consequently, it is hypothesized that FELICS's grouped, corrected estimate exceeds the atomic estimate from the same joint run for both concepts, i.e., $\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{joint}}(\mathbf{x},A) > \mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{atomic}}(\mathbf{x},A)$ and $\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{joint}}(\mathbf{x},B) > \mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{atomic}}(\mathbf{x},B)$, using the notation to be introduced in Section~\ref{sec:controlled_atomic_baseline}, and that this relationship holds consistently across all evaluated models.
-
-\end{enumerate}
+The preceding chapters have modeled the proposed extension FELICS from a purely theoretical standpoint: not only were the principles guiding its design established in Chapter~\ref{sec:conception}, but its behavior was also formalized and predicted in Chapter~\ref{sec:modeling_updated_metric}. However, it remains to be shown whether the metric behaves in practice as its theoretical construction suggests. Furthermore, empirical data is needed to answer the guiding research questions formulated in Chapter \ref{sec:research_questions}. To this end, an evaluation is conducted against a reference implementation, whose methodology is prepared within this chapter.
 
 \section{Evaluation Settings}
 The research questions are investigated under a common set of implementation and sampling decisions. The following subsections document the exact settings used to make the experiments reproducible and their results comparable.

 \subsubsection{New German Credit}
 \label{sec:new_german_credit_dataset}
-The German Credit dataset and its updated version, South German Credit \cite{grompingSouthGermanCredit2019}, are widely used within the machine learning community. These datasets consist of tabular information describing credit applicants at a German bank during the 1970s, together with the final decision made by bank employees regarding whether the credit application was approved. One notable aspect of this data is the correlated nature of several columns. However, the outdated nature of both datasets---for instance, the use of the obsolete \emph{Deutsche Mark} currency, or telephone ownership as a recorded property---may confuse modern LLMs. A synthetic dataset inspired by German Credit was therefore constructed specifically for this evaluation. Several columns where manually defined and specified with their intended meanings and value ranges. DeepSeek R1 was then instructed to generate 200 fictional credit-applicant records with plausible values and associations between related columns. These specifications were generation objectives rather than an empirical calibration to current economic conditions in Germany. DeepSeek R1 was chosen because it is neither of the two evaluated models, reducing the direct overlap between the model generating the data and those evaluated on it. The creation prompt is reproduced in Appendix~\ref{app:prompts}, Prompt~\ref{prompt:new_german_credit_generation}. The resulting dataset is available as \texttt{.csv}-file in the FELICS source code, within the \path{/data} subfolder.

 \subsection{Selection of Models}
-Causal Concept Faithfulness, as proposed by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, is designed to be compatible with an arbitrary LLM, a strength that FELICS preserves. Selecting concrete models for evaluation is therefore inherently difficult. Based on these criteria, two medium-sized, state-of-the-art LLMs were selected.
+\label{sec:selection_models}
+Causal Concept Faithfulness, as proposed by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, is designed to be compatible with an arbitrary LLM, a strength that FELICS preserves. Selecting concrete models for evaluation is therefore inherently difficult. This decision was guided by the constraint that a given model must either be hostable on local hardware or accessible via a free API with reasonable usage limits, owing to cost constraints. Based on these criteria, two medium-sized, state-of-the-art LLMs were selected. They were chosen to be comparable in size but to differ in developer, architecture and training data. With only two models, the evaluation cannot attribute differences in faithfulness to a single model property such as scale or architecture. Instead, it examines whether FELICS leads to consistent conclusions for models that have little in common. Two sizes of one model family would share training data and alignment procedures, and because GPT-OSS 120B also serves as $\mathcal{A}$, a smaller GPT-OSS variant would intensify the circularity concern discussed in Section~\ref{sec:matton_limitations}. Matching the parameter count also removes scale as the most obvious explanation for differences between the two models.
 
-Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} evaluate \texttt{GPT-3.5}, \texttt{GPT-4o}, and \texttt{Claude~3.5 Sonnet}. Although reusing these models would facilitate direct reproduction of their experimental setting, their proprietary, paid API access was not feasible within the resources available for this thesis; the evaluation therefore uses open-weight models that can be executed locally or accessed without comparable usage costs.
+Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} evaluate GPT-3.5, GPT-4o, and Claude~3.5 Sonnet. It serves as an example of a current American reasoning model and is executed on local infrastructure.

+\subsubsection{Auxiliary LLM}
+GPT-OSS 120B serves not only as a model under evaluation but also as the auxiliary LLM responsible for concept extraction from questions, interaction presumption, counterfactual generation, and concept extraction from the generated explanations across all experimental runs.
+
+While employing the same model for both generation ($\mathcal{M}$) and auxiliary parsing ($\mathcal{A}$) departs from the recommendation in Section~\ref{sec:matton_limitations} and may raise concerns regarding evaluation circularity and bias transfer, this approach directly mirrors the methodology established by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}. In their work, GPT-4o was similarly utilized as the universal auxiliary model, including in runs where it was the model under evaluation. This decision was further supported by pilot experiments, which demonstrated that GPT-OSS 120B possessed the highest reliability for these complex extraction tasks. Additionally, beyond practical hardware advantages such as reducing the number of models that must be concurrently loaded on the hosting machine, this configuration provides a unified baseline that enables observations regarding cross-model (mis-)alignment between $\mathcal{M}$ and $\mathcal{A}$.
 
 \subsection{Samples per Question}
-Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} collected 50 samples per original and counterfactual question in their own work. While this number of samples improves the quality of the resulting estimates, it is infeasible within the time and resource constraints of this thesis.
+Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} collected 50 samples per original and counterfactual question in their work ($S = 50$). While a sample size of this scale improves estimate reliability, it is unfeasible within the time and resource constraints of this thesis. Given the available hardware, generating a single complete output for a dataset question response or auxiliary task on a local model (even a smaller variant such as Mistral Small 24B) typically requires at least 40 seconds. Because FELICS also requires more completions for both response generation and auxiliary tasks than the original framework (see Equation~\ref{eq:subset_counterfactual_bound}), a reduced sample size is necessary.
+
+Therefore, for both models, $S=10$ responses are gathered for BBQ and MedQA. For New German Credit, the number of samples will be further reduced to $S=5$, since each question is conceptually identical and differs only in its underlying data, suggesting higher statistical stability.
+
+\subsection{Question Selection from Datasets}
+\label{sec:questions_per_dataset}
+The reduced number of samples per question is, in part, a deliberate trade-off: it frees the computational budget to keep the number of evaluated questions per dataset and model combination comparatively high. Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} evaluate 30 questions per dataset in their work. The same number is therefore adopted here, so that $N=30$ questions are evaluated for each combination of dataset and model.
 
-Therefore, for both models, \textbf{$S=10$} responses are gathered for BBQ and MedQA. Although this constitutes only a fifth of the sample size used by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, it is sufficient to obtain meaningful results. For New German Credit, the number of samples will be further reduced to \textbf{$S=5$}, since each question is conceptually identical and differs only in its underlying data, suggesting higher statistical stability.
+Wherever possible, the questions themselves are selected in the same way as by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, so that results on the natural-language datasets remain directly comparable. For BBQ, Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} use the first $N$ questions in the order in which they appear in the dataset source; the same questions are evaluated here. For MedQA, they instead select specific question indices to obtain a representative set of questions; these indices are adopted unchanged. For New German Credit, which was constructed for this thesis and therefore has no prior selection, the first $N$ rows of the generated dataset are evaluated. This deterministic rule avoids any manual selection of records by the author. The exact selection arguments and question identifiers for all datasets are documented in Appendix~\ref{app:reproducibility}. Appendix~\ref{app:referenced-questions} summarizes every question that is discussed individually.
+
+Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} further observe that dataset-level faithfulness scores remain stable once approximately 15 questions are included. It is, however, uncertain to what extent this observation transfers to the present evaluation. With fewer responses per question, each question-level estimate is subject to greater sampling variation, so a larger number of questions may be required before dataset-level results stabilize.
+
+An alternative allocation of the same budget would have been to double the number of responses per question while restricting the evaluation to $N=15$ representative questions per dataset. This option was not pursued, because the two sources of uncertainty affect different parts of the analysis: the number of responses per question governs the precision of individual answer distributions and CE estimates, whereas the number of questions determines the basis for population-level inference. Since the hypothesis tests specified in Section~\ref{sec:analysis_procedures} operate on question-level observations, halving the number of questions to $N=15$ would substantially reduce statistical power and widen the uncertainty of every test, whereas the additional responses per question would not compensate for this loss. Retaining $N=30$ questions therefore provides a more reliable foundation for the statistical analyses than a smaller set of more precisely estimated questions.
 
 \subsection{Removal vs.\ Value-Replacement Counterfactuals}
-For MedQA, Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} use counterfactuals in which the value of the concept under intervention is \emph{removed}. They argue that, within a medical context, verifying the plausibility of alternative values generated by the auxiliary LLM is difficult for non-experts, and that determining a meaningful alternative value is not always possible for certain factors (e.g., blood pressure). Conversely, for the experiments on the BBQ dataset, Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} use \emph{value replacements}, in which a property takes on \emph{one} new value.
+For MedQA, Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} use counterfactuals in which the value of the concept under intervention is removed. They argue that, within a medical context, verifying the plausibility of alternative values generated by the auxiliary LLM is difficult for non-experts, and that determining a meaningful alternative value is not always possible for certain factors (e.g., blood pressure). Conversely, for the experiments on the BBQ dataset, Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} use value replacements, in which a property takes on one new value. Although Equation~\ref{eq:ce} formally averages over all plausible alternative values in $\mathbb{C}'_m$, Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} restrict this set to a single alternative value per concept for value replacements, i.e.,~$|\mathbb{C}'_m|=1$. This constitutes a substantial simplification for evaluation, since the resulting CE reflects the effect of one specific replacement rather than the average effect across all plausible alternatives. For comparability and due to resource constraints, this work adopts the same restriction and evaluates exactly one alternative value per concept for value replacements.
 
-Although it would be interesting to evaluate alternative intervention approaches, it has been decided, for the sake of comparability and given the limited available resources, that the evaluation is conducted using exactly the same counterfactual-generation approach as Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} for each respective dataset. The newly introduced New German Credit dataset is assessed using \emph{concept removal}, since the absence of potentially conflicting information (as discussed in Section~\ref{sec:influence}) may help to better reveal the interaction effects of certain concepts.
+Although it would be interesting to evaluate alternative intervention approaches, it has been decided, for the sake of comparability and given the limited available resources, that the evaluation is conducted using exactly the same counterfactual-generation approach as Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} for each respective dataset. The newly introduced New German Credit dataset is assessed using \emph{concept removal}. Value replacements can introduce implausible or contradictory information into a question: if the \textit{Applicant Age} of a 69-year-old applicant whose \textit{Job Category} is \enquote{Retired} were replaced by 24, for instance, the resulting input would describe a 24-year-old retiree, a combination that is unusual at best and likely rare in the evaluated models' training data. Such inconsistencies become more likely when several concepts are modified jointly and can obscure the measured causal effects. Removing concept values avoids these conflicts and may therefore help to better reveal the interaction effects of certain concepts.
 
 \subsection{Explanations Used to Estimate Explanation-Implied Effects}
 \label{sec:ee_evaluation_setup}
-The source of the explanations used to estimate the explanation-implied-effect (EE) vector depends on the intervention strategy. For the removal-based datasets MedQA and New German Credit, the EE vector is estimated exclusively from explanations generated for the original, unmodified question. Explanations generated for removal counterfactuals are not included in this estimate. For BBQ, which uses value-replacement counterfactuals, explanations from both the original and counterfactual questions are used in accordance with Equation~\ref{eq:ee}.
+The source of the explanations used to estimate the EE vector depends on the intervention strategy. For the removal-based datasets MedQA and New German Credit, the EE vector is estimated exclusively from explanations generated for the original, unmodified question. Explanations generated for removal counterfactuals are not included in this estimate. For BBQ, which uses value-replacement counterfactuals, explanations from both the original and counterfactual questions are used in accordance with Equation~\ref{eq:ee}.
 
 This dataset-specific choice follows the experimental procedure reported by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}. It is also required by the semantics of concept removal: once a concept has been removed from the question, a valid explanation of the resulting answer cannot generally be expected to identify that concept as influential. Including such counterfactual explanations would therefore reduce the estimated EE for reasons introduced by the intervention itself rather than because the original explanation failed to reflect the model's behavior. This concern is particularly pronounced for FELICS because a joint intervention may remove several concepts simultaneously, thereby mechanically restricting the set of concepts that can legitimately occur in the counterfactual explanation. Counterfactual responses remain necessary for estimating causal effects from changes in the answer distribution; the restriction applies only to the explanations used to construct the EE vector.

 \label{sec:analysis_procedures}

The corresponding scripts can be found in the \path{/evaluation} subfolder of the FELICS repository introduced in Section~\ref{sec:reference_implementation}.
+Whereas Chapter~\ref{sec:research_questions} specifies what each research question asks and states the corresponding hypothesis, this section operationalizes the relevant constructs and defines the quantitative or qualitative procedure used to assess each hypothesis under the experimental settings established above.
 
 \subsection{Evaluation Conditions and Causal-Effect Notation}
 \label{sec:controlled_atomic_baseline}
 
 For each dataset and evaluated model, three runs supply the causal-effect results: the original implementation of Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, which has no oracle; FELICS with the oracle restricted to $n_{\max}=1$; and a joint FELICS run with $n_{\max}=4$ for BBQ and MedQA or $n_{\max}=3$ for New German Credit. The result paths for these runs are documented in Appendix~\ref{app:reproducibility}. The runs collect their data separately and can therefore differ in their sampled responses, explanations, and counterfactuals. For natural-language inputs, they may also identify different concept sets.
 
+\noindent\textbf{Definitions and estimation.} The CE and faithfulness quantities defined in this section, like those in Equations~\ref{eq:ce} and~\ref{eq:faithfulness}, specify the estimands: they determine which data enter a score and how concepts, groups, and runs are combined. The reported values are not obtained by evaluating these expressions on raw empirical frequencies. Instead, as in Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} and described in Section~\ref{sec:matton_evaluation_pipeline}, all CE and faithfulness scores are posterior estimates from the Bayesian hierarchical models, fitted separately to the data of each run and calculation. These estimates are expected to closely approximate the defined quantities but need not coincide with them exactly, particularly for questions with few concepts or responses.
+
 \noindent\textbf{Four causal-effect quantities.} The joint run supports two calculations: one using only singleton effects and one attributing the effects of joint interventions. Together with the other two runs, this gives four quantities. Their dataset and model arguments are omitted because each comparison fixes both.

 \subsection{Interaction-Presumption Strength and Causal-Effect Changes}
-This subsection operationalizes \rqref{rq:correlation_diff}, introduced in Section~\ref{sec:research_questions}.

 \noindent\textbf{Scope and comparison.} The relationship between the strength of the oracle's interaction presumption and the resulting CE difference is assessed separately for each evaluated model on the natural-language datasets BBQ and MedQA.  \noindent\textbf{Operationalization.} For every question $\mathbf{x}$ with concept set $\mathbf{C}$, the interaction-presumption strength under configuration $n_{\max}$ is defined as
 \begin{equation}
-	\kappa(\mathbf{x}, n_{\max}) := \frac{1}{|\mathbf{C}|} \sum_{s \,\in\, \mathcal{O}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{C},n_{\max})} \ \sum_{\emptyset \neq T \subseteq s} |T|,
+	\kappa(\mathbf{x}, n_{\max}) := \frac{1}{|\mathbf{C}|} \sum_{s \,\in\, \mathbf{S}(\mathbf{x})} \ \sum_{\emptyset \neq T \subseteq s} |T|,
 \end{equation}
-that is, the total number of concept occurrences across all nonempty treatments evaluated for $\mathbf{x}$, normalized by the number of concepts $|\mathbf{C}|$. +Using $\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{atomic}}$ and $\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{joint}}$ as defined in Equations~\ref{eq:evaluation_ce_atomic} and~\ref{eq:evaluation_ce_joint}, the question-level CE difference averaged across all concepts $\mathbf{C}$ in $\mathbf{x}$ is
 \begin{equation}
 	\Delta \mathrm{CE}(\mathbf{x}) := \frac{1}{|\mathbf{C}|}\sum_{C_m\in\mathbf{C}}
 	\left|\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{joint}}(\mathbf{x},C_m)-\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{atomic}}(\mathbf{x},C_m)\right|.
 \end{equation}
