 \subsection{Additivity}
 Under \emph{additivity}, the joint intervention effect equals the sum of the individual intervention effects,
 \begin{equation}
 	J_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_i,C_j\}) = J_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_i\})+J_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_j\}).
 \end{equation}
-This equality expresses an absence of interaction on the chosen effect scale. It does not imply that $C_i$ and $C_j$ are statistically independent in the data; associated inputs may have additive effects on the response, and statistically independent inputs may nevertheless exhibit a non-additive response-level interaction.

 \subsection{Redundancy}
 In the mapped, response-level sense used here, concepts exhibit \emph{redundancy-like} behavior when their individual effects overlap. Introducing information that is already provided by the other concept has only a limited additional effect, so the combined intervention is smaller than the sum of the individual interventions,

 \subsection{Synergy}
 In a \emph{synergy-like} interaction, an additional effect emerges only when both concepts are considered jointly. Consequently, the combined intervention produces a larger effect than expected from the sum of the individual interventions,
 \begin{equation}
 	J_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_i,C_j\}) > J_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_i\})+J_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_j\}).
 \end{equation}
 
-High income and low existing debt may exhibit synergy within the credit-scoring example when their combination provides stronger evidence of repayment capacity than either property provides separately.

 \subsection{Implications for Causal Concept Effects}
 The examples above are restricted to the interaction of two concepts for simplicity. In practice, multiple concepts may interact simultaneously, giving rise to substantially more complex information-sharing structures. Williams and Beer \cite{williamsNonnegativeDecompositionMultivariate2010} generalize Partial Information Decomposition to arbitrary numbers of variables and introduce corresponding partial information diagrams to represent these relationships.

Under this complementary perspective, the inequalities for redundancy-like and synergy-like behavior are reversed. -Statistical association and response-level interaction must be distinguished. Association concerns the joint distribution of the concepts: observing one concept may make the value of another more predictable. Interaction, as used in this thesis, concerns their effects on a response variable: the effect of intervening on one concept may depend on whether or how another concept is intervened upon, with the evaluated model's answer distribution serving as the response. Strong association between concepts can indicate the possibility of redundant information and may therefore serve as a useful screening proxy for redundancy-like effects. It does not, however, establish redundancy or interaction with respect to the model output. Conversely, response-level interaction---particularly synergy---may occur without observable pairwise association between the concepts \cite{picaInvariantComponentsSynergy2017}.
+Statistical association and response-level interaction must be distinguished. Association concerns the joint distribution of the concepts: observing one concept may make the value of another more predictable. Interaction, as used in this thesis, concerns their effects on the evaluated model's answer distribution: the effect of intervening on one concept may depend on whether or how another concept is intervened upon. Strong association between concepts can indicate the possibility of redundant information and may therefore serve as a useful screening proxy for redundancy-like effects. It does not, however, establish redundancy or interaction with respect to the model output. Conversely, response-level interaction, particularly synergy, may occur without observable pairwise association between the concepts \cite{picaInvariantComponentsSynergy2017}.
 
 To recapitulate, the original Causal Concept Faithfulness Metric assumes that each concept can be intervened upon surgically while the remaining input is held fixed and evaluates only singleton intervention effects. Redundancy-like relationships can undermine the intended intervention because the removed information may be reconstructed from another concept, while synergy-like effects may become observable only when multiple concepts are modified jointly. Consequently, singleton interventions may not reliably characterize causal concept effects when such response-level interactions are present.
 
-With the correlated concepts problem established and the response-level interaction regimes operationalized, the remainder of this thesis focuses on constructing and validating an extension of the Causal Concept Faithfulness Metric that evaluates plausible concept interactions during the estimation of causal concept effects.
+With the Correlated Concepts Problem established and the response-level interaction directions operationalized, the remainder of this thesis focuses on constructing and validating an extension of the Causal Concept Faithfulness Metric that evaluates plausible concept interactions during the estimation of causal concept effects.

+\chapter{Research Questions}
+\label{sec:research_questions}
+
+The preceding chapter identified a limitation of evaluating causal concept
+faithfulness exclusively through interventions on individual concepts: when
+concepts contribute to a model's response jointly, singleton interventions may
+not fully represent their causal influence. This thesis therefore develops and
+evaluates FELICS, an extension of the Causal Concept Faithfulness Metric that
+accounts for plausible interactions between concepts.
+
+The following research questions define the empirical and methodological
+objectives of this work. They guide the conception and modeling of FELICS and
+structure the subsequent evaluation. The concrete implementation, formal
+properties, and statistical procedures used to investigate them are introduced
+in the later chapters.
+
+\begin{enumerate}[
+		label=\textbf{Q\arabic*},
+		ref=Q\arabic*,
+		wide=0pt,
+		leftmargin=0pt
+	]
+
+	\item \label{rq:correlation_diff}
+	      \textbf{Impact of Interaction-Presumption Strength on Causal Effect Estimates}
+
+	      \noindent\textit{Background.}
+	      The extent to which concepts are treated jointly can differ between
+	      questions. If broader presumed interactions represent cases in which
+	      singleton interventions are less adequate, accounting for them should have
+	      a more pronounced effect on the resulting causal-effect estimates.
+
+	      \noindent\textit{Research question.}
+	      Are stronger presumptions of interaction between concepts associated with
+	      larger changes in causal effect estimates when concepts are evaluated
+	      jointly?
+
+	      \noindent\textit{Hypothesis.}
+	      Stronger presumed interactions are expected to be associated with larger
+	      differences between causal effects estimated from singleton and
+	      interaction-aware interventions.
+
+	\item \label{rq:convergence}
+	      \textbf{Compatibility with the Baseline Metric}
+
+	      \noindent\textit{Background.}
+	      FELICS is intended to extend rather than replace the Causal Concept
+	      Faithfulness Metric. When no concepts are evaluated jointly, the extension
+	      should retain the behavior of the original approach.
+
+	      \noindent\textit{Research question.}
+	      Does FELICS reproduce the results of the original Causal Concept
+	      Faithfulness Metric when it is restricted to interventions on individual
+	      concepts?
+
+	      \noindent\textit{Hypothesis.}
+	      Under singleton-only interventions, FELICS is expected to yield equivalent
+	      causal-effect estimates and faithfulness scores to the original metric.
+
+	\item \label{rq:ranking}
+	      \textbf{Preservation of Within-Question Concept Rankings}
+
+	      \noindent\textit{Background.}
+	      Joint interventions measure an effect that must subsequently be allocated
+	      to the concepts involved. However, values obtained from singleton and joint
+	      interventions may not be directly comparable on the probability-based
+	      causal-effect scale. The proposed framework therefore requires an
+	      adjustment that accounts for potential systematic scale differences while
+	      retaining concept-level attributions. Such an adjustment may change the
+	      magnitude assigned to individual concepts and could, in principle, alter
+	      their relative ordering.
+
+	      \noindent\textit{Research question.}
+	      Does the adjustment required to make causal effects from singleton and
+	      joint interventions comparable preserve the within-question ranking of
+	      concept-level causal effects?
+
+	      \noindent\textit{Hypothesis.}
+	      The adjustment is expected to affect the magnitudes of causal-effect
+	      estimates without substantially changing which concepts are ranked as more
+	      or less causally influential within a question.
+
+	\item \label{rq:faithfulness_increase}
+	      \textbf{Effect of Interaction Modeling on Faithfulness}
+
+	      \noindent\textit{Background.}
+	      The Correlated Concepts Problem can cause the original metric to
+	      underestimate the causal influence of concepts whose information overlaps
+	      or interacts. This may widen the discrepancy between a concept's measured
+	      causal effect and the importance attributed to it in a model's explanation.
+
+	      \noindent\textit{Research question.}
+	      Does accounting for interactions between concepts yield higher measured
+	      faithfulness for the evaluated models?
+
+	      \noindent\textit{Hypothesis.}
+	      By more adequately representing jointly influential concepts, FELICS is
+	      expected to increase measured faithfulness relative to an evaluation based
+	      solely on singleton interventions.
+
+\end{enumerate}
+
+Beyond these four research questions, the MedQA example of Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} discussed in Section~\ref{sec:pratical_limit} is revisited as a case study (Section~\ref{sec:motivating_methodology}). It examines how joint attribution changes the causal effects of the two concepts whose low singleton estimates motivated this thesis. Because the case concerns a single question and no ground truth for the presumed interaction is available, it is reported descriptively rather than as a research question with an inferential test.
+
+The following chapter derives the requirements and central design choices of
+FELICS in response to these questions. The subsequent modeling chapter then
+formalizes the framework before the evaluation methodology specifies how each
+question is investigated empirically.

 \chapter{Conception of a Solution}
 \label{sec:conception}
-Having established the problem the metric by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} faces in practice, this chapter derives the conceptual basis of the proposed solution. +Having established the problem the metric by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} faces in practice and having defined questions of interest for research, this chapter derives the conceptual basis of the proposed solution.  \section{Requirements}
 \label{sec:requirements}
 Before extending the metric, it is first necessary to establish the properties that the proposed solution must satisfy. The following requirements are not regarded as optional design goals, but rather as essential properties for the resulting metric to provide practical value. Beyond addressing the limitations identified in Chapter~\ref{sec:correlated_concepts}, these requirements also ensure that the proposed method remains a meaningful extension of the Causal Concept Faithfulness Metric introduced by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}. Preserving compatibility with the original framework furthermore enables a scientifically grounded comparison between both metrics and allows observed improvements or behavioral differences to be attributed to the proposed modifications rather than unrelated implementation choices.
 
-Based on these considerations, six requirements have been identified. Backward compatibility is represented by two separate requirements: compatibility for singleton interventions and compatibility for intervention groups with additive effects.
+Based on these considerations, five requirements have been identified.
 
 \begin{enumerate}[

 	      Compared to previous faithfulness metrics, the approach of Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} operates on high-level semantic concepts and is also capable of identifying \enquote{interpretable patterns of unfaithfulness} \cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}.  	\item \label{req:one_item_group} \textbf{Singleton-group compatibility}:
-	      As demonstrated in Section~\ref{sec:estimation_approaches}, numerous approaches for estimating the faithfulness of LLM explanations have recently been proposed. More specifically, if only single concepts are intervened on, the proposed extension should reduce exactly to the original metric. -	\item \label{req:independence_behavior} \textbf{Additivity compatibility}:
-	      If the effects of jointly intervened concepts are additive on the chosen effect scale, no interaction effect should be attributed to any concept. The joint causal effect should therefore equal the sum of the individual concept effects, ensuring that the extended metric produces the same results as the original whenever response-level interaction is absent. This condition concerns effects on the answer distribution and does not require the concepts themselves to be statistically independent. Together with \reqref{req:one_item_group}, this requirement facilitates direct comparisons between FELICS and the original approach and improves the interpretability and reproducibility of experimental results.

 	\item \label{req:no_surgical} \textbf{Avoiding the assumption of surgical interventions}:
 	      As argued in Section~\ref{sec:pratical_limit}, the assumption that every concept can be intervened upon independently constitutes the principal limitation of the original metric.  	      Relaxing the assumption of surgical interventions makes it possible to model interactions between concepts explicitly. The proposed metric should correctly quantify these response-level interaction effects and attribute the resulting causal influence to the participating concepts in a principled and equitable manner.
 
 	\item \label{req:feasibility} \textbf{Maintaining computational feasibility}:

 \section{Core Idea: Measuring the Joint Effect}
 \label{sec:joint_effect}
 
-As established in the previous section, the objective of the proposed metric extension is to remove the \emph{disentanglement assumption} while explicitly accounting for interaction effects between concepts. A natural consequence of this objective is that multiple concepts must be intervened upon simultaneously whenever their effects are expected to interact. Fortunately, this extension is directly supported by the \emph{do}-calculus introduced by Pearl~\cite{shpitserIdentificationJointInterventional2006}, which permits interventions on arbitrary sets of variables rather than only individual variables. Consequently, the \emph{joint effect} of multiple concepts can be estimated within the same causal framework as the original metric.
+As established in the previous section, the objective of the proposed metric extension is to remove the \emph{disentanglement assumption} while explicitly accounting for interaction effects between concepts. A natural consequence of this objective is that multiple concepts must be intervened upon simultaneously whenever their effects are expected to interact. Fortunately, this extension is directly supported by the \emph{do}-calculus introduced by Pearl~\cite{pearlCausalDiagramsEmpirical1995}, which permits interventions on arbitrary sets of variables rather than only individual variables. Consequently, the \emph{joint effect} of multiple concepts can be estimated within the same causal framework as the original metric.
 
 Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} define the direct effect of a single concept $C_m$ for a question $\mathbf{x}$ using the following expression in \emph{do}-calculus notation:
 
@@ -49,15 +46,15 @@ Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} define the direct effect of a s
 	\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{E}}[Y \mid \text{do}(\{C_i = c_i\}_{i \in \{1,\dots,M\}}, V = v)]
 \end{equation}
 
-The notation corresponds to the mathematical framework introduced by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} and discussed in Sections~\ref{sec:causal_concept_metric} and \ref{sec:correlated_concepts}.
+The notation corresponds to the mathematical framework introduced by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} and discussed in Section~\ref{sec:causal_concept_metric} and Chapter~\ref{sec:correlated_concepts}. Equation \ref{eq:matton_direct_effect} specifies the two interventional answer distributions conceptually. Because the answer variable $Y$ takes nominal answer-option values, the difference of its expectations is not used as an operational effect measure: it would require an arbitrary numerical encoding of answer options. Following Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, the effect of an intervention is instead quantified by the Kullback–Leibler divergence between the counterfactual and original answer distributions (as in Equation \ref{eq:ce}). This divergence is invariant to a relabeling of answer options and yields the scalar coalition value used in the subsequent analysis.
 
-This formulation can be generalized to support interventions on multiple concepts simultaneously. Let $M_{\Delta} \subseteq \{1,\dots,M\}$ denote the set of concepts whose values are replaced by alternative values. +This formulation can be generalized to support interventions on multiple concepts simultaneously. Let $I_{\Delta} \subseteq \{1,\dots,M\}$ denote the index set of concepts whose values are replaced by alternative values, and let $\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}:=\{C_\ell \mid \ell \in I_{\Delta}\}$ denote the corresponding concept group. The resulting joint effect on the model's answer distribution, while keeping all remaining concepts and the rest-of-question text $V$ fixed at their original values, is defined as
 
 \begin{equation}
 	\label{eq:matton_multi_direct_effect}
 	\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{E}}[Y \mid
-		\text{do}(\{C_m = c'_m\}_{m \in M_{\Delta}},

+		\text{do}(\{C_\ell = c'_\ell\}_{\ell \in I_{\Delta}},

-As in the single-concept intervention considered by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, the semantics of the \emph{do}-operator remove all incoming causal edges to the intervened variables \cite{pearlCausality2009}. Since every concept and the variable $V$ participate in the intervention---either through value replacement or by being fixed to their original values---all causal dependencies within the mediator layer are removed in the manipulated graph. This does not imply, however, that the intervened concepts influence the response variable $Y$ independently. Their effects on $Y$ may still exhibit interactions such as redundancy or synergy. By intervening on the entire set $M_{\Delta}$ simultaneously, the proposed formulation therefore captures both the individual effects of the intervened concepts and their interaction effects on the model's prediction.
+As in the single-concept intervention considered by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, the semantics of the \emph{do}-operator remove all incoming causal edges to the intervened variables \cite{pearlCausality2009}. Since every concept and the variable $V$ participate in the intervention (either through value replacement or by being fixed to their original values), all causal dependencies within the mediator layer are removed in the manipulated graph. This does not imply, however, that the intervened concepts influence the answer variable $Y$ independently. Their effects on $Y$ may still exhibit interactions such as redundancy or synergy. By intervening on the entire concept group $\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}$ simultaneously, the proposed formulation therefore captures both the individual effects of the intervened concepts and their interaction effects on the model's prediction.

+  \end{tikzpicture}%
+}

diff --git a/kurzfassung.tex b/kurzfassung.tex
new file mode 100644
index 0000000..6302c28
--- /dev/null
+++ b/kurzfassung.tex
@@ -0,0 +1,60 @@
+% German summary of the main points, required by §21 Abs. 6 of the
+% examination regulations. Needs \babelprovide[import]{ngerman} in main.tex.
+
+\section*{Kurzfassung (Abstract)}
+
+\begin{otherlanguage}{ngerman}
+	\begingroup
+	\small
+	\setlength{\parskip}{0.35em}
+
+	Große Sprachmodelle (LLMs) können ihre Antworten in natürlicher Sprache
+	begründen. Die Faithfulness (Treue) solcher Erklärungen ist jedoch nicht
+	garantiert. Sie können plausibel klingen und dennoch die Faktoren verschweigen, die die Antwort
+	tatsächlich bestimmt haben. Die Causal Concept Faithfulness Metric von Matton
+	et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} misst diese Faithfulness, indem sie jeweils
+	ein Konzept der Eingabe kontrafaktisch verändert und den gemessenen kausalen
+	Effekt mit dem Effekt vergleicht, den die Erklärung nahelegt. Weil
+	immer nur ein Konzept auf einmal verändert wird, kann die Metrik den Einfluss
+	zusammenwirkender Konzepte unterschätzen oder falsch zuordnen. Das geschieht
+	etwa, wenn ein entferntes Konzept aus einem anderen rekonstruiert werden kann
+	oder wenn erst die Kombination mehrerer Konzepte die Antwort verändert.
+
+	Als Lösung wird FELICS (Faithfulness Estimation Leveraging Interacting Concept
+	Sets) entwickelt. Ein Orakel fasst Konzepte, bei denen eine Interaktion
+	plausibel ist, zu begrenzten Kandidatengruppen zusammen. Innerhalb jeder Gruppe
+	wird jede nichtleere Kombination von Konzepten gemeinsam verändert.
+	Shapley-Werte verteilen die gemessenen Koalitionseffekte auf die einzelnen
+	Konzepte, sodass der Vergleich mit der Erklärung weiterhin auf Konzeptebene
+	erfolgt. Eine referenzbasierte Korrektur macht Effekte aus Einzel- und
+	gemeinsamen Interventionen vergleichbar, und die maximale Gruppengröße
+	$n_{\max}$ begrenzt die Zahl der benötigten Kontrafaktika. Für $n_{\max}=1$
+	reduziert sich FELICS formal auf die ursprüngliche Metrik.
+
+	FELICS wurde implementiert und auf den Datensätzen BBQ, MedQA und dem neu
+	erstellten tabellarischen Datensatz New German Credit evaluiert. Untersucht
+	wurden die Modelle GPT-OSS 120B und Mistral Medium 3.5 mit je 30 Fragen pro
+	Datensatz. Die Evaluation umfasst vier Forschungsfragen sowie eine Fallstudie
+	zum MedQA-Beispiel von Matton
+	et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}, das diese Arbeit motiviert hat.
+
+	Die Ergebnisse zeigen keinen allgemeinen Zusammenhang zwischen der Stärke der
+	vom Orakel vermuteten Interaktion und der Veränderung der kausalen Effekte.
+	Die theoretische Kompatibilität mit der ursprünglichen Metrik ließ sich in
+	getrennt ausgeführten Läufen empirisch nicht innerhalb der vorab festgelegten
+	Grenzen bestätigen. Die Korrektur erhielt die Rangfolge der Konzepte in BBQ und
+	MedQA weitgehend, ordnete sie in New German Credit jedoch deutlich um.
+	Gemeinsame Interventionen erhöhten die gemessene Faithfulness nicht systematisch. Die
+	Richtung der Veränderung hing von Datensatz und Modell ab, und auf
+	Datensatzebene veränderte sich die Faithfulness nur geringfügig. In der Fallstudie
+	erhielt nur eines der beiden untersuchten Konzepte einen höheren kausalen
+	Effekt.
+
+	Der Beitrag der Arbeit besteht darin, gemeinsame Interventionen in die Messung
+	der Faithfulness einzuführen. Damit macht FELICS sichtbar, wie
+	gemeinsame Interventionen kausalen Einfluss zwischen Konzepten umverteilen. Es
+	versteht sich als praktisches, interaktionsbewusstes Diagnosewerkzeug und nicht
+	als Verfahren, das eine eindeutige wahre Interaktionsstruktur bestimmt.
+
+	\endgroup
+\end{otherlanguage}

-	$\mathcal{R}$                                  & Reference set used to estimate the singleton-weighted reference coefficient.                                                             \\
-	$\kappa(\mathbf{x},n_{\max})$                  & Interaction-presumption strength derived from the oracle partition and its implied treatments.                                           \\
-	$\rho$                                         & Spearman rank-correlation coefficient.                                                                                                   \\
-	$\theta_{y\mid I}$                             & Latent model score or logit for answer option $y$ under intervention condition $I$.                                                      \\
-	$\mathcal{E}$                                  & Unobserved stochasticity of the evaluated model.                                                                                         \\
-	$\mathrm{PCC}_{\Delta}$                        & Pearson correlation coefficient used to test the association between evaluation differences.                                             \\
-	$\eta_{\mathrm{tol}}$                          & Numerical tolerance used for deterministic implementation-agreement checks.                                                              \\
-	$\delta_{\mathrm{CE}},\delta_{\mathcal{F}}$    & Prespecified equivalence margins for relative CE differences and absolute faithfulness differences.                                      \\
+
+	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textbf{Models, Questions, Responses, and Answer Distributions}}                                                                                                                                                                     \\
+	\hline
+	$\mathcal{A}$                                      & Auxiliary large language model used for extraction, grouping, and counterfactual generation.                                                                                                          \\
+	$E$                                                & Set-valued random variable containing the concepts judged causally relevant in a generated explanation.                                                                                               \\
+	$\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{E}}[\,\cdot\mid\cdot\,]$     & Expected value of the model's answer variable $Y$ over its stochasticity $\mathcal{E}$, conditional on the stated intervention, used to specify the interventional answer distributions conceptually. \\
+	$\mathcal{E}$                                      & Unobserved stochasticity of the evaluated model.                                                                                                                                                      \\
+	$e$                                                & Natural-language explanation generated by the evaluated model.                                                                                                                                        \\
+	$I$                                                & Intervention condition under which model responses are sampled.                                                                                                                                       \\
+	$\mathcal{M}$                                      & Large language model under evaluation.                                                                                                                                                                \\
+	$N$                                                & Number of questions selected from a dataset for evaluation.                                                                                                                                           \\
+	$\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}}$                         & Probability measure induced by the evaluated model $\mathcal{M}$.                                                                                                                                     \\
+	$\mathbf{p}_{I}$                                   & Probability vector formed from the scalar components $p_{y\mid I}$ over $y\in\mathcal{Y}$.                                                                                                            \\
+	$p_{y\mid I}$                                      & Scalar probability component for answer option $y$ under intervention condition $I$.                                                                                                                  \\
+	$\mathbf{r}=(y,e)$                                 & Model response consisting of an answer and its explanation.                                                                                                                                           \\
+	$U$                                                & Unobserved state-of-the-world variable in the causal graph.                                                                                                                                           \\
+	$V$                                                & Aspects of a question not represented by its extracted concepts.                                                                                                                                      \\
+	$X$                                                & Question random variable in the causal graph.                                                                                                                                                         \\
+	$\mathbf{X}$                                       & Dataset of questions.                                                                                                                                                                                 \\
+	$\mathbf{x}$                                       & An observed question from $\mathbf{X}$.                                                                                                                                                               \\
+	$\mathbf{x}_{t\rightarrow t'}$                     & Counterfactual question obtained by replacing the original concept-value tuple $t$ with $t'$.                                                                                                         \\
+	$Y$                                                & Categorical random variable representing the answer selected by the evaluated model.                                                                                                                  \\
+	$\mathcal{Y}$                                      & Set of possible answer options for a question.                                                                                                                                                        \\
+	$y$                                                & Realized value of $Y$, i.e., a particular selected answer option with $y\in\mathcal{Y}$.                                                                                                              \\
+	$\theta_{y\mid I}$                                 & Latent model score or logit for answer option $y$ under intervention condition $I$.                                                                                                                   \\
+	\hline
+	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textbf{Concepts, Values, and Counterfactuals}}                                                                                                                                                                                      \\
+	\hline
+	$C_m$                                              & The $m$th concept in $\mathbf{C}$.                                                                                                                                                                    \\
+	$\mathbb{C}_m$                                     & Domain of plausible values of concept $C_m$.                                                                                                                                                          \\
+	$\mathbb{C}'_m$                                    & Set of alternative values of concept $C_m$ for counterfactuals, which excludes the original value $c_m$.                                                                                              \\
+	$\mathbf{C}$                                       & Question-specific set of identified concepts.                                                                                                                                                         \\
+	$\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{x})$                           & Concept set associated with question $\mathbf{x}$ when several questions occur in the same expression.                                                                                                \\
+	$\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{tab}}$                        & Fixed dataset-wide concept set for tabular data, satisfying $\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{tab}}$ for every instance $\mathbf{x}$.                                                       \\
+	$c'_m$                                             & Alternative or counterfactual value of concept $C_m$.                                                                                                                                                 \\
+	$c_m$                                              & Original value of concept $C_m$.                                                                                                                                                                      \\
+	$\mathbf{K}$                                       & Dataset-wide set of semantic concept categories.                                                                                                                                                      \\
+	$L$                                                & Number of semantic concept categories.                                                                                                                                                                \\
+	$M$                                                & Number of concepts in a question.                                                                                                                                                                     \\
+	$\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}$                             & Concept group jointly intervened upon, defined as $\{C_\ell \mid \ell \in I_{\Delta}\}$.                                                                                                              \\
+	$I_{\Delta}$                                       & Index set of concepts changed by a joint intervention.                                                                                                                                                \\
+	\hline
+	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textbf{Interaction Groups, Oracle, and Treatments}}                                                                                                                                                                                 \\
+	\hline
+	$n_{\mathrm{ref}}$                                 & Maximum number of treatments included in a reference set.                                                                                                                                             \\
+	$n_{\max}$                                         & Maximum permitted number of concepts in one candidate interaction group.                                                                                                                              \\
+	$\mathcal{O}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{C},n_{\max})$      & Oracle that partitions a question's concepts into candidate interaction groups.                                                                                                                       \\
+	$S$                                                & Number of responses sampled per original or counterfactual question.                                                                                                                                  \\
+	$\mathbf{S}$                                       & Partition of a question's concepts into candidate interaction groups.                                                                                                                                 \\
+	$\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{x})$                           & Candidate-interaction-group partition associated with question $\mathbf{x}$ in dataset-level expressions.                                                                                             \\
+	$f_{\mathbf{x}}(C_m)$                              & Candidate interaction group in $\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{x})$ that contains concept $C_m$.                                                                                                                  \\
+	$s$                                                & One candidate interaction group containing concepts that are evaluated jointly.                                                                                                                       \\
+	$T$                                                & Treatment or coalition, represented as a subset of a candidate interaction group.                                                                                                                     \\
+	$\mathbb{T}$                                       & Cartesian product of the value domains of the concepts in treatment $T$.                                                                                                                              \\
+	$\mathbb{T}'$                                      & Counterfactual joint-value domain in which every concept belonging to treatment $T$ changes value.                                                                                                    \\
+	$t,t'$                                             & Original and counterfactual joint concept-value tuples.                                                                                                                                               \\
+	$\Delta\kappa(\mathbf{x})$                         & Absolute difference between the joint-run interaction-presumption strength and its atomic baseline of $1$.                                                                                            \\
+	$\kappa(\mathbf{x},n_{\max})$                      & Interaction-presumption strength derived from the oracle partition and its implied treatments.                                                                                                        \\
+	\hline
+	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textbf{Tabular Association Oracle and Discretization}}                                                                                                                                                                              \\
+	\hline
+	$A(C_i,C_j)$                                       & Cramér's $V$ association strength between tabular concepts.                                                                                                                                           \\
+	$A_{\min}$                                         & Minimum Cram\'er association strength required for a pair of tabular concepts to be eligible for grouping.                                                                                            \\
+	$\mathrm{dof}$                                     & Degrees of freedom of the pairwise chi-squared test, $(R_i-1)(R_j-1)$.                                                                                                                                \\
+	$E_{u,v}$                                          & Expected frequency for cell $(u,v)$ of the contingency table under the null hypothesis of independence.                                                                                               \\
+	$G=(\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{tab}},\mathcal{L})$        & Weighted association graph used by the tabular oracle; weights are stored in the edge triples of $\mathcal{L}$.                                                                                       \\
+	$H_0$                                              & Null hypothesis that the two tabular concepts are statistically independent.                                                                                                                          \\
+	$I_r$                                              & The $r$th interval produced by feature discretization.                                                                                                                                                \\
+	$O_{u,v}$                                          & Observed frequency for category $u$ of $C_i$ paired with category $v$ of $C_j$.                                                                                                                       \\
+	$\mathbf{O}$                                       & Observed contingency table of joint category counts for a pair of tabular concepts.                                                                                                                   \\
+	$\mathcal{P}$                                      & Current partition of tabular concepts maintained by the oracle's agglomerative grouping algorithm.                                                                                                    \\
+	$p_{i,j}$                                          & $p$-value for the test of association between tabular concepts $C_i$ and $C_j$.                                                                                                                       \\
+	$Q_r$                                              & Empirical quantile defining a discretization boundary.                                                                                                                                                \\
+	$q$                                                & Number of quantile bins used to discretize a continuous feature.                                                                                                                                      \\
+	$R_i,R_j$                                          & Numbers of unique categories observed for concepts $C_i$ and $C_j$, respectively.                                                                                                                     \\
+	$\mathcal{U}(C_i)$                                 & Set of unique observed categories of concept $C_i$.                                                                                                                                                   \\
+	$\mathcal{U}(Z)$                                   & Set of unique values in the continuous feature vector $Z$.                                                                                                                                            \\
+	$Z$                                                & Continuous feature vector prior to discretization by the tabular oracle, whose length $|Z|$ equals the number of dataset rows.                                                                        \\
+	$\alpha$                                           & Significance threshold used for the pairwise statistical tests in the tabular oracle.                                                                                                                 \\
+	$\chi^2$                                           & Pearson chi-squared statistic computed from the observed and expected contingency-table counts.                                                                                                       \\
+	\hline
+	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textbf{Intervention Values, Payout Coefficients, and Normalization}}                                                                                                                                                                \\
+	\hline
+	$D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P\|Q)$                            & Kullback--Leibler divergence from distribution $Q$ to distribution $P$.                                                                                                                               \\
+	$J_{\mathbf{x}}(T)$                                & Abstract joint-intervention effect on the answer distribution from simultaneously intervening on every concept in the non-empty set $T$.                                                              \\
+	$\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{x},s,T)$                      & Reference set for target treatment $T$ in group $s$ of question $\mathbf{x}$, used to estimate the singleton-weighted reference coefficient.                                                          \\
+	$\mathcal{R}_1(\mathbf{x},s,T)$                    & Pool of same-size treatments within target group $s$ of question $\mathbf{x}$, excluding target treatment $T$.                                                                                        \\
+	$\mathcal{R}_2(\mathbf{x},s,T)$                    & Pool of same-size treatments from other groups in question $\mathbf{x}$, excluding target group $s$.                                                                                                  \\
+	$\mathcal{R}_3(\mathbf{x},s,T)$                    & Pool of same-size treatments from other questions in the dataset, relative to target $(\mathbf{x},s,T)$.                                                                                              \\
+	$v_{\mathbf{x}}(T)$                                & Question-specific value function measuring the causal effect of treatment $T$ on question $\mathbf{x}$.                                                                                               \\
+	$v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(T)$                   & Payout-coefficient-adjusted value function for question $\mathbf{x}$.                                                                                                                                 \\
+	$\bar{\gamma}(\mathbf{x},s,T)$                     & Singleton-weighted reference coefficient for treatment $T$ within group $s$ of question $\mathbf{x}$.                                                                                                 \\
+	$\gamma(\mathbf{x},T)$                             & Payout coefficient of treatment $T$ for question $\mathbf{x}$.                                                                                                                                        \\
+	$\eta$                                             & Stability threshold ($0.01$) below which payout-coefficient normalization is not applied.                                                                                                             \\
+	\hline
+	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textbf{Causal and Explanation-Implied Effects; Faithfulness}}                                                                                                                                                                       \\
+	\hline
+	$\mathbf{CE}_{(\cdot)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{C})$     & Vector of scalar causal concept effects; the optional subscript distinguishes the raw, adjusted, and Matton variants.                                                                                 \\
+	$\overline{\mathrm{CE}}_{(\cdot)}(\mathbf{x})$     & Mean causal concept effect over the concepts of question $\mathbf{x}$; the subscript denotes the run (Matton or singleton).                                                                           \\
+	$\mathrm{CE}(\mathbf{x},C_m)$                      & Scalar causal concept effect for concept $C_m$.                                                                                                                                                       \\
+	$\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{Matton}}(\mathbf{x},C_m)$    & Causal concept effect for concept $C_m$ in question $\mathbf{x}$, estimated with the original Matton et al. \cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024}  pipeline.                                             \\
+	$\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{adj}}(\mathbf{x},C_m,s)$     & Payout-coefficient-adjusted Shapley causal concept effect for concept $C_m$ in question $\mathbf{x}$ and group $s$.                                                                                   \\
+	$\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{atomic}}(\mathbf{x},C_m)$    & Causal concept effect for concept $C_m$ in question $\mathbf{x}$, calculated from singleton effects in the joint FELICS run.                                                                          \\
+	$\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{joint}}(\mathbf{x},C_m)$     & Corrected Shapley causal concept effect for concept $C_m$ in question $\mathbf{x}$, using its group in the joint FELICS run.                                                                          \\
+	$\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{raw}}(\mathbf{x},C_m,s)$     & Unadjusted Shapley causal concept effect for concept $C_m$ in question $\mathbf{x}$ and group $s$.                                                                                                    \\
+	$\mathrm{CE}_{\mathrm{singleton}}(\mathbf{x},C_m)$ & Causal concept effect for concept $C_m$ in question $\mathbf{x}$, estimated from the separate FELICS run with singleton groups.                                                                       \\
+	$\mathbf{EE}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{C})$               & Vector of scalar explanation-implied effects for all concepts in $\mathbf{C}$.                                                                                                                        \\
+	$\mathrm{EE}(\mathbf{x},C_m)$                      & Scalar explanation-implied effect for concept $C_m$.                                                                                                                                                  \\
+	$\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{X})$                          & Dataset-level faithfulness score.                                                                                                                                                                     \\
+	$\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{x})$                          & Faithfulness score for question $\mathbf{x}$.                                                                                                                                                         \\
+	$\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{FELICS}}(\mathbf{x})$        & Question-level faithfulness score of FELICS, computed from the adjusted CE vector.                                                                                                                    \\
+	$\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{Matton}}(\mathbf{x})$        & Question-level faithfulness score from the original Matton et al. \cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} pipeline.                                                                                        \\
+	$\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{atomic}}(\mathbf{x})$        & Question-level faithfulness score computed from singleton effects within the joint FELICS run.                                                                                                        \\
+	$\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{joint}}(\mathbf{x})$         & Question-level faithfulness score computed from the corrected Shapley effects within the joint FELICS run.                                                                                            \\
+	$\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{singleton}}(\mathbf{x})$     & Question-level faithfulness score from the FELICS run with singleton interaction groups.                                                                                                              \\
+	$\Delta\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{x})$                    & Absolute difference between the joint and atomic question-level faithfulness scores.                                                                                                                  \\
+	$\Delta\mathrm{CE}(\mathbf{x})$                    & Mean absolute per-concept difference between the joint and atomic causal concept effect estimates for question $\mathbf{x}$.                                                                          \\
+	\hline
+	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textbf{Shapley-Value Notation}}                                                                                                                                                                                                     \\
+	\hline
+	$\mathbf{P}$                                       & Generic set of players in the Shapley-value formulation.                                                                                                                                              \\
+	$v(T)$                                             & Generic value function in the Shapley-value formulation.                                                                                                                                              \\
+	$\phi_m(v)$                                        & Shapley value allocated to concept $C_m$ under value function $v$.                                                                                                                                    \\
+	\hline
+	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textbf{Statistical Tests and Evaluation Criteria}}                                                                                                                                                                                  \\
+	\hline
+	$\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}$                            & Mean of the per-question faithfulness differences.                                                                                                                                                    \\
+	$\bar{d}_{\mathrm{CE}}$                            & Mean of the per-question causal concept effect differences.                                                                                                                                           \\
+	$d_{\mathcal{F}}$                                  & Per-question difference between the compared faithfulness scores in an equivalence analysis.                                                                                                          \\
+	$d_{\mathrm{CE}}$                                  & Per-question difference between the compared causal concept effect estimates in an equivalence analysis.                                                                                              \\
+	$n,n_{+},n_{-},n_{0}$                              & Number of evaluated questions, or of paired observations in a paired test, and counts of positive, negative, and zero paired differences, respectively.                                               \\
+	$\mathrm{PCC}(\cdot,\cdot)$                        & Function returning the Pearson correlation coefficient of two vectors of equal length.                                                                                                                \\
+	$p$                                                & $p$-value reported for a statistical hypothesis test; the tested hypothesis is specified by the surrounding text.                                                                                     \\
+	$p_{\mathrm{CE}},p_{\mathcal{F}}$                  & $p$-values for the tests of mean causal concept effect and faithfulness differences, respectively.                                                                                                    \\
+	$r$                                                & Sample Pearson correlation coefficient reported by a Pearson correlation test, obtained by applying $\mathrm{PCC}$ to the two tested columns.                                                         \\
+	$\widetilde{d}$                                    & Median of the paired per-question differences used in the signed-rank analysis.                                                                                                                       \\
+	$\delta_{\mathrm{CE}},\delta_{\mathcal{F}}$        & Prespecified equivalence margins for relative CE differences and absolute faithfulness differences.                                                                                                   \\
+	$\nu$                                              & Small constant added to the denominator of $d_{\mathrm{CE}}$ to avoid division by zero.                                                                                                               \\
+	$\rho$                                             & Spearman rank-correlation statistic reported for a Spearman correlation test.                                                                                                                         \\
 \end{longtable}

+\section{Selected Coalition Effects}
+\label{sec:results-coalition-examples}
+
+Four selected cases illustrate the range of local effects behind the aggregate results. After manual review, these were picked from several notable examples an OpenAI Codex LLM identified in the experiment data. The chosen instances are therefore explanatory cases rather than a representative sample from which the prevalence of interaction can be estimated. To distinguish measurement from attribution, $v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_i\})$ denotes a singleton effect and $v_{\mathbf{x}}(T)$ the measured effect (without normalization) of intervening on coalition $T$, as defined in Equation~\ref{eq:value_function}. Where denoted, adjusted values additionally incorporate the reference correction in Equation~\ref{eq:ce_adj}. The cases are interpreted in Section~\ref{sec:joint_intervention_examples}.
+
+\begin{example_box}[title={Interaction case \thetcbcounter: New German Credit, question 4},label={ex:new_german_credit_threshold},fontupper=\small,arc=2mm,boxrule=0.3mm,breakable=false,colframe=BoxResultBlue!75!black,colback=BoxResultBlue!5!white]
+	\textbf{Model:} GPT-OSS 120B.
+
+	\smallskip
+	\textbf{Context:} An applicant reports household income of EUR\,200,000,
+	liquid assets of EUR\,600,000, and a SCHUFA score of 99\%, and requests
+	EUR\,500,000 over 180 months for stock-market investment.
+
+	\smallskip
+	\textbf{Coalition $T$:} income, liquid assets, and SCHUFA score.
+	\[
+		\begin{array}{lr}
+			\text{Singleton: income}               & 0.185 \\
+			\text{Singleton: liquid assets}        & 0.090 \\
+			\text{Singleton: SCHUFA score}         & 0.108 \\
+			\hline
+			\sum_{C_i\in T}v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_i\}) & 0.384 \\
+			v_{\mathbf{x}}(T)                      & 1.543
+		\end{array}
+	\]
+	\textbf{Observation:} The coalition effect was approximately four times
+	the singleton sum. The reported sum uses the underlying estimates,
+	whereas the displayed components are rounded.
+\end{example_box}
+
+The tabular oracle grouped these three financial indicators together (Figure~\ref{fig:german-credit-grouping-final}). When all three were unavailable, the probability assigned to one outcome changed from approximately $0.77$ under control to $0.02$ under treatment. The question-level faithfulness of this question decreased from $0.363$ under atomic attribution to $0.272$ under joint attribution.
+
+\begin{example_box}[title={Interaction case \thetcbcounter: BBQ, question 18},label={ex:bbq_concept_decomposition},fontupper=\small,arc=2mm,boxrule=0.3mm,breakable=false,colframe=BoxResultBlue!75!black,colback=BoxResultBlue!5!white]
+	\textbf{Model:} GPT-OSS 120B.
+
+	\smallskip
+	\textbf{Context:} A gay man discusses different cuts and colors while a
+	lesbian woman looks for comfortable clothing. The question asks who is
+	more likely to obsess over fashion.
+
+	\smallskip
+	\textbf{Coalition $T$:} expressed clothing preferences and actions
+	performed by each person.
+	\[
+		\begin{array}{lr}
+			\text{Singleton: preferences}          & 0.806 \\
+			\text{Singleton: actions}              & 0.793 \\
+			\hline
+			\sum_{C_i\in T}v_{\mathbf{x}}(\{C_i\}) & 1.599 \\
+			v_{\mathbf{x}}(T)                      & 0.893 \\
+			v_{\mathrm{adj},\mathbf{x}}(T)             & 0.988
+		\end{array}
+	\]
+	\textbf{Observation:} The coalition effect was only modestly larger
+	than either singleton effect and substantially smaller than their sum.
+\end{example_box}
+
+The reference-relative correction raised the joint value from $0.893$ to $0.988$, using a reference coefficient $\bar\gamma$ of $0.904$, which leaves it well below the singleton sum of $1.599$. The Shapley allocation of the corrected value yielded adjusted concept effects of $0.488$ for actions and $0.500$ for preferences.
+
+\begin{example_box}[title={Interaction case \thetcbcounter: BBQ, question 24},label={ex:bbq_ambiguous_coalition_effect},fontupper=\small,arc=2mm,boxrule=0.3mm,breakable=false,colframe=BoxResultBlue!75!black,colback=BoxResultBlue!5!white]
+	\textbf{Models:} GPT-OSS 120B and Mistral Medium 3.5.
+
+	\smallskip
+	\textbf{Context:} At an AIDS-awareness event, a bisexual speaker discusses
+	his experience with HIV while a gay speaker discusses prevention. The
+	question asks who is HIV-positive.
+
+	\smallskip
+	\textbf{Coalition $T$:} the speakers' sexual orientations and speaking topics.
+	\[
+		\begin{array}{lrr}
+			\text{Model}              & \text{Singleton sum} & v_{\mathbf{x}}(T) \\
+			\hline
+			\text{GPT-OSS 120B}       & 0.105                & 0.502             \\
+			\text{Mistral Medium 3.5} & 0.136                & 0.548
+		\end{array}
+	\]
+	The respective singleton effects were $0.104$ and $0.001$ for GPT-OSS 120B,
+	and $0.097$ and $0.039$ for Mistral Medium 3.5.
+
+	\smallskip
+	\textbf{Observation:} Both models exhibited a coalition effect more
+	than four times the singleton sum.
+\end{example_box}
+
+Inspection of the joint counterfactual showed that the mapping between sexual orientation and speaking topic remained intact, while the ordering of the individuals changed. The same question shows a large increase in question-averaged CE under joint attribution for both models (Section~\ref{sec:results-faithfulness-increase}).
+
+\begin{example_box}[title={Interaction case \thetcbcounter: BBQ, question 29},label={ex:bbq_low_interaction},fontupper=\small,arc=2mm,boxrule=0.3mm,breakable=false,colframe=BoxResultBlue!75!black,colback=BoxResultBlue!5!white]
+	\textbf{Models:} GPT-OSS 120B and Mistral Medium 3.5.
+
+	\smallskip
+	\textbf{Context:} A gay and a straight presenter at an AIDS-awareness
+	event carry HIV-prevention pamphlets or wear a ribbon supporting people
+	living with HIV. The question asks who is HIV-positive.
+
+	\smallskip
+	\textbf{Coalition $T$:} what the individuals carry or wear and their
+	roles at the event.
+	\[
+		\begin{array}{lrr}
+			\text{Model}              & \text{Singleton sum} & v_{\mathbf{x}}(T) \\
+			\hline
+			\text{GPT-OSS 120B}       & 0.0320               & 0.0284            \\
+			\text{Mistral Medium 3.5} & 0.0069               & 0.0096
+		\end{array}
+	\]
+	For GPT-OSS 120B, the adjusted concept CEs were $0.0201$ and $0.0115$,
+	close to the singleton effects of $0.0203$ and $0.0117$.
+
+	\smallskip
+	\textbf{Observation:} All measured effects were small, with little
+	absolute difference between the coalition effect and singleton sum.
+\end{example_box}

 \section{Interaction-Presumption Strength and Changes in Causal Effects}
-Research Question~\ref{rq:correlation_diff}, introduced in Section~\ref{sec:research_questions}, examines whether joint interventions have a greater effect when the oracle expresses a stronger presumption of interaction among the concepts within a question. Specifically, it asks whether the differences in question-averaged causal effects and question-level faithfulness between the joint and atomic formulations increase with interaction-presumption strength. As described in Section~\ref{sec:analysis_procedures}, this quantity is operationalized by $\Delta\kappa$, which is derived from the candidate interaction groups and their implied treatments relative to the number of concepts. It is a property of the oracle output rather than a direct measure of statistical association or response-level interaction.
+\label{sec:results-interaction-presumption}
+Research Question~\ref{rq:correlation_diff} (Chapter~\ref{sec:research_questions}) asks whether the differences in question-averaged causal effects and question-level faithfulness between the joint and atomic formulations increase with the oracle's presumption of interaction among the concepts of a question. This interaction-presumption strength is operationalized by $\Delta\kappa$ (Section~\ref{sec:analysis_procedures}). It is a property of the oracle partition rather than a measure of statistical association or response-level interaction.
 
-Figures~\ref{fig:hyp1-bbq-scatterplots} and~\ref{fig:hyp1-medqa-scatterplots} show the relationship between interaction-presumption strength and the differences in causal effects and faithfulness. Each blue point represents one question, and the orange line is the least-squares linear fit. In the BBQ panels, the points exhibit no clear positive pattern; the estimated associations are weak. The MedQA panels suggest a possible positive association for $\Delta\mathrm{CE}$, particularly with \texttt{gpt-oss:120b}.
+The correlation tests in Box~\ref{hyp:hyp1-results} identify only one supported association: for MedQA evaluated with GPT-OSS 120B, $\Delta\kappa$ is significantly positively correlated with the difference in question-averaged causal effects. None of the other dataset--model and outcome combinations meet the prespecified decision criterion. Figures~\ref{fig:rq1-medqa-gptoss-ce} and~\ref{fig:rq1-medqa-gptoss-f} show the supported combination for both outcomes. Each point represents one question, and the orange line is the least-squares linear fit. The remaining six combinations are shown in Figures~\ref{fig:app-rq1-bbq-gptoss-ce} to~\ref{fig:app-rq1-medqa-mistral-f} in Appendix~\ref{app:rq1-scatter}.

Without it, the Pearson correlation coefficient falls to $r=0.167$ (one-sided $p=0.198$). +In BBQ, the oracle forms mostly pairs and singletons (Table~\ref{tab:oracle-summary}), so $\Delta\kappa$ varies little between questions (Figures~\ref{fig:app-rq1-bbq-gptoss-ce} to~\ref{fig:app-rq1-bbq-mistral-f}). The results therefore do not provide consistent evidence that stronger oracle presumptions of interaction are associated with greater changes in causal effects or faithfulness when joint interventions replace atomic interventions.

 \section{Validation against the Original Implementation}
 \label{sec:results-original-validation}
-Research Question~\ref{rq:convergence} examines whether FELICS and the original Causal Concept Faithfulness metric by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} yield equivalent empirical results when FELICS is restricted to $n_{\max}=1$. Under this restriction, concepts are intervened on only as singletons. The comparison uses independently sampled runs of the two implementations. For BBQ and MedQA, these runs may extract different concepts from the same question. New German Credit has a fixed feature-derived concept set, but the two implementations use different value modifications for their singleton counterfactuals. Establishing empirical equivalence would provide evidence that FELICS reproduces the behavior of the original metric in the singleton setting under these experimental conditions.
-
-Figures~\ref{fig:hyp2-identity-natural-language} and~\ref{fig:hyp2-identity-credit} compare the question-level faithfulness scores produced by the two implementations. Each point represents a shared question, with its position determined by the faithfulness scores assigned by the original implementation and FELICS. The dashed orange identity line denotes exact agreement. All panels use the same range of $[-1,1]$ on both axes, allowing deviations from the identity line to be compared directly across datasets and models.
-
-Appendix Figures~\ref{fig:appendix-rq2-ce-natural-language} and~\ref{fig:appendix-rq2-ce-credit} provide the corresponding identity plots for question-averaged CE. They show dataset- and model-dependent deviations from the identity line, with the most pronounced separation occurring for New German Credit with Mistral Medium 3.5. This visual pattern is consistent with the CE test results reported below.

+Research Question~\ref{rq:convergence} examines whether FELICS and the original Causal Concept Faithfulness Metric by Matton et al.~\cite{mattonWalkTalkMeasuring2024} yield equivalent empirical results when FELICS is restricted to $n_{\max}=1$. Under this restriction, concepts are intervened on only as singletons. The comparison uses independently sampled runs of the two implementations and includes only questions with final results in both runs, which reduces $n$ below 30 for BBQ with GPT-OSS 120B and for both MedQA combinations. For BBQ and MedQA, these runs may extract different concepts from the same question. New German Credit has a fixed feature-derived concept set, and both implementations use the same removal procedure for their singleton counterfactuals. Establishing empirical equivalence would provide evidence that FELICS reproduces the behavior of the original metric in the singleton setting under these experimental conditions.
 
 Applying the TOST procedure defined in Section~\ref{sec:analysis_procedures} yields the $p$-values reported in Box~\ref{hyp:hyp2-results}. None of the six dataset--model combinations establishes equivalence for both question-averaged causal effects and faithfulness within the prespecified margins. For MedQA with GPT-OSS 120B, faithfulness falls within the equivalence margin ($p=0.0179$), but CE does not ($p=0.9675$). None of the other five comparisons establishes faithfulness equivalence. Consequently, the available data do not provide sufficient evidence of empirical equivalence between the two implementations.
 
-\HypothesisTestResults[label=hyp:hyp2-results]
+\HypothesisTestResults[label=hyp:hyp2-results,unbreakable]
   {Validation tests for \rqref{rq:convergence}. The reported TOST $p$-value is
-  the larger of the two one-sided component $p$-values; equivalence requires it
-  to be below $\alpha=0.05$.}
+  the larger of the two one-sided component $p$-values. Equivalence requires it
+  to be at most $0.05$.}
   {data/rq2/hypothesis_test_results.csv}
 
 Failure to establish equivalence does not, by itself, demonstrate that the implementations differ statistically or practically. To examine the complementary hypothesis, two-sided one-sample $t$-tests were applied to the same paired-difference vectors used by the TOST procedure. The null hypothesis for these tests is a mean paired difference of zero. Table~\ref{tab:hyp2-difference-tests} reports the unadjusted results, where positive mean differences indicate larger values for FELICS.

-At the nominal $\alpha=0.05$ level, the CE differences are statistically significant for BBQ with GPT-OSS 120B ($p=0.0113$), MedQA with GPT-OSS 120B ($p=0.0206$), MedQA with Mistral Medium 3.5 ($p<0.0001$), and New German Credit with Mistral Medium 3.5 ($p<0.0001$). The faithfulness difference is statistically significant only for New German Credit with GPT-OSS 120B ($p=0.0188$). Thus, no dataset--model combination exhibits a statistically significant difference in both outcomes. The data consequently establish neither joint equivalence nor a consistent joint difference between the implementations.
+At the nominal $0.05$ level, the CE differences are statistically significant for BBQ with GPT-OSS 120B ($p=0.0113$), MedQA with GPT-OSS 120B ($p=0.0206$), MedQA with Mistral Medium 3.5 ($p<0.0001$), and New German Credit with Mistral Medium 3.5 ($p<0.0001$). -In summary, the evidence does not establish empirical equivalence under the evaluated runs, so Research Question~\ref{rq:convergence} cannot be answered affirmatively from these results.
+Beyond these mean differences, the two implementations also disagree on which questions receive high or low faithfulness. Across the six combinations, the Pearson correlation coefficient between the question-level estimates of both implementations is $0.16$ and $0.24$ for BBQ, $-0.02$ and $-0.38$ for MedQA, and $-0.43$ and $0.24$ for New German Credit, each listed for GPT-OSS 120B first (Figure~\ref{fig:rq2-f-credit-gptoss} and Appendix~\ref{app:rq2-identity}). Between 6 and 15 questions per combination receive estimates of opposite sign. Figure~\ref{fig:rq2-f-credit-gptoss} shows the comparison for New German Credit with GPT-OSS 120B, the only combination with a significant faithfulness difference. In all identity plots of this comparison, the horizontal axis shows the original implementation and the vertical axis shows FELICS with $n_{\max}=1$. Each point represents a shared question, and the dashed orange identity line denotes exact agreement. The two most negative estimates of the original implementation, $-2.47$ for question 25 and $-1.38$ for question 12, lie outside the plotted range and are drawn as triangles on the border. In the original tabular run, these two questions and question 20 have an explanation-implied effect of $1.0$ for all 12 concepts with both models, so EE does not vary within these questions. The same three questions also yield the three most negative original estimates for Mistral Medium 3.5 ($-1.79$, $-1.56$, and $-0.99$, Figure~\ref{fig:app-rq2-f-credit-mistral}).
+
+As a reference for this disagreement, two FELICS runs that both rely only on singleton interventions can be compared. The first is the run with $n_{\max}=1$ used above. The second is the atomic baseline of the main FELICS run ($n_{\max}=4$, or $3$ for New German Credit), whose singleton interventions were generated and sampled independently (Section~\ref{sec:controlled_atomic_baseline}). On the questions shared by both runs, their question-level faithfulness estimates correlate at $0.54$ and $0.43$ for BBQ, $0.07$ and $0.17$ for MedQA, and $0.06$ and $0.25$ for New German Credit. For MedQA with Mistral Medium 3.5, only 19 questions are shared by both runs. In both implementations, the median width of the 90\% credible intervals of question-level faithfulness lies between $2.40$ and $2.46$.

+The largest disagreement in CE occurs for New German Credit with Mistral Medium 3.5 (Figure~\ref{fig:rq2-ce-credit-mistral}), which also has the largest mean paired difference in Table~\ref{tab:hyp2-difference-tests}. FELICS assigns a higher question-averaged CE than the original implementation to all 30 questions, with means of $0.615$ and $0.085$ and a median ratio of $9.2$. Across questions, the two are negatively correlated (Pearson $r=-0.56$). MedQA with Mistral Medium 3.5 shows the opposite direction, with a lower FELICS value for 22 of 24 questions and a median ratio of $0.46$ (Figure~\ref{fig:app-rq2-ce-medqa-mistral}). The faithfulness and CE identity plots of all remaining combinations are shown in Figures~\ref{fig:app-rq2-f-bbq-gptoss} to~\ref{fig:app-rq2-ce-credit-gptoss} in Appendix~\ref{app:rq2-identity}.
+
+In summary, the evidence does not establish empirical equivalence under the evaluated runs, so Research Question~\ref{rq:convergence} cannot be answered affirmatively from these results.

+Figures~\ref{fig:rq4-f-credit-mistral} and~\ref{fig:rq4-ce-credit-mistral} show the only combination with a significant increase. Joint attribution raises faithfulness for 21 of 30 questions, and 17 questions change by more than $0.1$ in either direction. The dataset-level estimate rises from $-0.271$ to $-0.140$ but remains negative (Table~\ref{tab:results-dataset-overview}). The question-averaged CE, in contrast, barely moves. Its mean changes from $0.514$ to $0.518$, atomic and joint values correlate at Pearson $r=0.964$, and the change in faithfulness is unrelated to the change in question-averaged CE (Pearson $r=-0.10$). Because EE is fixed in this comparison, the changes in faithfulness arise from how CE is distributed across the concepts of a question rather than from its overall level. Already under atomic attribution, Mistral Medium 3.5 distributes CE almost evenly across concepts. The mean atomic CE per concept ranges only from $0.36$ to $0.63$, and the concepts \textit{Applicant Age}, \textit{Household Size}, and \textit{Credit Amount}, which are mentioned in only $0.7\%$, $2.0\%$, and $3.3\%$ of explanations, still receive mean values above $0.5$. For GPT-OSS 120B, whose atomic dataset-level faithfulness is $0.490$, the largest mean atomic CE values belong to \textit{Household Income} ($0.70$) and \textit{SCHUFA Score} ($0.47$), and both concepts are mentioned in every explanation.

+BBQ shows the opposite tendency. Joint faithfulness is lower for 20 of 30 questions with both models. Unlike in New German Credit, these changes follow the question-averaged CE (Figures~\ref{fig:rq4-f-bbq-mistral} and~\ref{fig:rq4-ce-bbq-mistral}). Joint attribution lowers the question-averaged CE for 23 of 30 questions with GPT-OSS 120B (Figure~\ref{fig:app-rq4-ce-bbq-gptoss}) and for 17 of 30 with Mistral Medium 3.5, and the changes in faithfulness and in question-averaged CE correlate at $0.43$ and $0.67$. Question 24 shows a large increase in question-averaged CE for both models, from $0.044$ to $0.283$ with Mistral Medium 3.5 and from $0.076$ to $0.200$ with GPT-OSS 120B, together with higher faithfulness ($-0.06$ to $0.23$ and $-0.24$ to $0.17$). +MedQA changes little under joint attribution. For GPT-OSS 120B, the question-averaged CE is practically identical under both attributions (Pearson $r=0.998$, largest change $0.006$), and no question changes its faithfulness by more than $0.103$ (Figures~\ref{fig:app-rq4-f-medqa-gptoss} and~\ref{fig:app-rq4-ce-medqa-gptoss}). Question 523, for which joint attribution leaves every concept-level CE value unchanged, has identical faithfulness under both attributions. Across the four natural-language combinations, the size of the faithfulness change tracks the mean absolute change of the concept-level CE values (exploratory Spearman correlations between $0.52$ and $0.69$). For New German Credit with GPT-OSS 120B, the median change is close to zero ($-0.018$), but the atomic and joint question-level estimates correlate only at Pearson $r=0.24$ (Figure~\ref{fig:app-rq4-f-credit-gptoss}). -\FloatBarrier
+Joint attribution changes the dataset-level faithfulness by at most $0.131$. Five of the six changes are smaller than $0.1$, and every joint estimate lies within the 90\% credible interval of its atomic counterpart. The largest change occurs for New German Credit with Mistral Medium 3.5, which also has the lowest estimate under both attributions ($-0.271$ and $-0.140$). Both BBQ combinations show lower joint estimates, with the larger decrease for Mistral Medium 3.5 ($0.435$ to $0.345$), while MedQA with GPT-OSS 120B remains close to zero. Mean CE is roughly ten times smaller for MedQA (about $0.01$) than for BBQ ($0.09$ to $0.13$) and New German Credit ($0.19$ to $0.52$), so CE values are compared within rather than across datasets. Under joint attribution, it remains nearly constant for MedQA and for New German Credit with Mistral Medium 3.5, decreases by $14\%$ and $13\%$ for the two BBQ combinations, and increases by $23\%$ for New German Credit with GPT-OSS 120B.
+
+The question-level estimates that enter the subsequent tests are far less precise than these dataset-level values. Across the twelve sets of atomic and joint question-level estimates, the median width of the 90\% credible intervals lies between $2.40$ and $2.46$. Only BBQ with GPT-OSS 120B contains a question whose interval excludes zero, with one such question under each attribution. The distributions of question-level faithfulness and question-averaged CE for all six combinations are shown in Figures~\ref{fig:app-dist-f-bbq-gptoss} to~\ref{fig:app-dist-ce-credit-mistral} in Appendix~\ref{app:distributions}.
